\frac{\;}{\mathsf{precise}(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil)}\par\frac{\mathsf{precise}(\varphi,\psi)}{\mathsf{precise}(\varphi\ast\psi)}\par\frac{\mathsf{precise}(\varphi)\hskip 10.00002pt\varphi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\mapsto X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil}{\mathsf{precise}(\textstyle\bigoplus_{X\sim d(E)}\varphi)}

Almost sure assertions are always precise, since the smallest model is the one where
\llparenthesis P\rrparenthesis_{\Gamma}
occurs with probability 1, and is the smallest measurable set with nonzero probability. Separating conjunctions are precise if their subcomponents are, which follows from monotonicity of the independent product (
Lemma

B.4
). Outcome conjunctions are precise if the inner assertion is precise, and implies that
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\mapsto X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
for some program expression
e
, which witnesses how to partition the sample space for the direct sum.
Without this partitioning side condition, the result is not necessarily precise.
For example,
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\mapsto 1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\in\{0,1\}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
is not precise, since it is not possible to determine the probability of the event
x=1
, despite it being measurable in one of the sub-probability spaces.
However,
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\mapsto 1\ast y\mapsto 0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\in\{0,1\}\ast y\mapsto 1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
is precise, since
y
witnesses the partition.

Weak separating conjunctions
\mathop{\ast_{\mathsf{w}}}
and nondeterministic outcome conjunctions
\bignd
are
not
precise, as there are generally many different minimal probability spaces that satisfy them, assigning different probabilities to each event. However, those assertions do obey
convexity
, a weaker condition, which intuitively means that
\varphi\oplus_{p}\varphi\Rightarrow\varphi
. We give the formal definition below.

Definition 4.2 (Convex Assertions).

\mathsf{convex}(\varphi)
iff for all
\Gamma
under which
\varphi
is satisfiable, there exist
\Omega
,
\mathcal{F}
, and a convex set
S
of probability measures on
\mathcal{F}
such that:

\forall\mathcal{P}.\hskip 10.00002pt\Gamma,\mathcal{P}\vDash\varphi\hskip 20.00003pt\text{iff}\hskip 20.00003pt\exists\mu\in S.\hskip 10.00002pt\langle\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mu\rangle\preceq\mathcal{P}

Clearly any precise assertion is convex, since the set of measures
S
is just a singleton in that case. So, in addition to analogues of the precision rules above, we also have the following:

\frac{\mathsf{convex}(\varphi,\psi)}{\mathsf{convex}(\varphi\mathop{\ast_{\mathsf{w}}}\psi)}\par\frac{\mathsf{convex}(\varphi,\psi)\hskip 10.00002pt\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\mapsto 1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\hskip 8.19447pt\psi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}e\mapsto 0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\end{subarray}}{\mathsf{convex}(\varphi\oplus_{\geq p}\psi)}\par\frac{\mathsf{convex}(\varphi)\hskip 10.00002pt\varphi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\mapsto X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil}{\mathsf{convex}(\textstyle\bignd_{X\in E}\varphi)}

Precision and convexity are useful for formulating entailment laws, which we provide in
Figure

4
.
The first row consists of rules for the interaction between separating conjunctions and pure assertions. Weakening can be performed underneath both pure assertions and separating conjunctions. In addition, strong separation implies weak separation, and weak and strong separation are equivalent when one of the conjuncts is a pure assertion (since independence is trivial in that case).

\frac{P\vdash Q}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\vdash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Q\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil}\par\frac{\varphi\vdash\varphi^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\psi\vdash\psi^{\prime}}{\varphi\ast_{m}\psi\vdash\varphi^{\prime}\ast_{m}\psi^{\prime}}\par\varphi\ast\psi\vdash\varphi\mathop{\ast_{\mathsf{w}}}\psi\par\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\ast Q\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\dashv\vdash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast_{m}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Q\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\par\varphi\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\dashv\vdash\varphi\mathop{\ast_{\mathsf{w}}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\par\textstyle\bigoplus_{X\sim d(E)}\varphi\vdash\bignd_{X\in\mathsf{supp}(d(E))}\varphi\par\varphi[E/X]\dashv\vdash\bignd_{X\in\{E\}}\varphi\par\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}E\subseteq E^{\prime}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast\bignd_{X\in E}\varphi\vdash\bignd_{X\in E^{\prime}}\varphi\par\frac{\varphi\vdash\psi}{\textstyle\bigoplus_{X\sim d(E)}\varphi\vdash\bigoplus_{X\sim d(E)}\psi}\hskip 18.49988pt\frac{Y\notin\mathsf{fv}(\varphi)}{\textstyle\bigoplus_{X\sim d(E)}\varphi\vdash\bigoplus_{Y\sim d(E)}\varphi[Y/X]}\hskip 18.49988pt\frac{X\notin\mathsf{fv}(\psi)}{\textstyle(\bigoplus_{X\sim d(E)}\varphi)\ast\psi\vdash\bigoplus_{X\sim d(E)}(\varphi\ast\psi)}\par\frac{X\notin\mathsf{fv}(\psi)\hskip 7.6999pt\mathsf{precise}(\psi)}{\textstyle\bigoplus_{X\sim d(E)}(\varphi\ast\psi)\vdash(\bigoplus_{X\sim d(E)}\varphi)\ast\psi}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{X\notin\mathsf{fv}(\psi)\hskip 7.6999pt\mathsf{convex}(\psi)}{\textstyle\bigoplus_{X\sim d(E)}(\varphi\mathop{\ast_{\mathsf{w}}}\psi)\vdash(\bigoplus_{X\sim d(E)}\varphi)\mathop{\ast_{\mathsf{w}}}\psi}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{X\notin\mathsf{fv}(\varphi)\hskip 7.6999pt\mathsf{convex}(\varphi)}{\textstyle\bigoplus_{X\sim d(E)}\varphi\vdash\varphi}\par

Figure 4.
Selected entailment laws, where
\varphi[E/X]
denotes a syntactic substitution of
E
for
X
in
\varphi
. Recall that
m\in\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{w}\}
and when
m
is omitted,
\ast=\ast_{\mathsf{s}}
.

In the second row, we give some rules pertaining to
\bignd
. An outcome conjunction
\bigoplus_{X\sim d(E)}
can be weakened to a
\bignd
over
\mathsf{supp}(d(E))
, where
\mathsf{supp}(\mathbf{Ber}\left(E\right))\triangleq\{0,1\}
and
\mathsf{supp}(\mathbf{unif}\left(E\right))\triangleq E
. A
\bignd_{X\in\{E\}}\varphi
over a singleton set is the same as substituting
E
for
X
in
\varphi
. Finally, the bounds of a
\bignd
can always be expanded, similar to how
P\Rightarrow P\vee Q
in classical logic.

The final two rows pertain to outcome conjunctions, and each rule has a corresponding one (not shown) with
\bignd
instead of
\bigoplus
.
Weakening can be performed inside of an outcome conjunction, and bound variables can be
\alpha
-renamed as long as the new variable name is fresh. As in Bluebell, the (strong) separating conjunction distributes over the outcome conjunction, so that assertions can be moved inside of an outcome conjunction, but this rule is invalid if
\ast
is replaced by
\mathop{\ast_{\mathsf{w}}}
, due to the possibility of correlations between
\psi
and
d(E)
.

Factoring assertions out of an outcome conjunction is only supported in Bluebell for almost-sure assertions
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, whereas in
pcOL
, it can be performed for any
precise
assertion, due to our semantics based on a direct sum.
We saw at the end of
Section

4.2
that independent products (which model separating conjunctions) distribute over direct sums (which model outcome conjunctions), however the corresponding entailment
\bigoplus_{X\sim d(E)}(\varphi\ast\psi)\vdash(\bigoplus_{X\sim d(E)}\varphi)\ast\psi
requires that
\psi
is satisfied by the same model in each case, which can be guaranteed by forcing
\psi
to be precise. In fact, this exact scenario also arose in the
RCond
and
RCase
rules of
PSL
, where an analogous concept called
supported
was used to ensure soundness
(
Barthe
et al., 2020
)
. Replacing strong separation with weak separation, we only need
\psi
to be convex—not precise—to factor it out of the outcome conjunction, since independence is not implied. Finally, outcome conjunctions over convex assertions that do not depend on the bound variable
X
can be collapsed.

5.
Probabilistic Concurrent Outcome Logic

Probabilistic Concurrent Outcome Logic (
pcOL
) specifications are given as triples of the form
I\vDash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
, where
\varphi
and
\psi
are probabilistic assertions (
Section

4.3
),
C\in\mathsf{Cmd}
(
Figure

1
),
I
is a basic assertion, and
m\in\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{w}\}
. Roughly speaking, the meaning of these triples is that if the states are initially distributed according to
\varphi
, then any invariant sensitive execution of
C
with invariant
I
will satisfy
\psi
. The mode
m
dictates what kind of
frame preservation
property the triple has.

Recall from
Section

3.3
that invariant sensitive execution requires the invariant states to be drawn from a finite set. For this reason,
I
must be a
finitary
basic assertion; formally,
\mathsf{finitary}(I)
iff
\llparenthesis I\rrparenthesis_{\Gamma}
is a finite set for any context
\Gamma
.
The formal validity definition of
pcOL
triples is below.

Definition 5.1 (
pcOL
Triples).

The
pcOL
triple
I\vDash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
is valid iff for all
\Gamma\colon\mathsf{LVar}\to\mathsf{Val}
,
\mu
,
and probability spaces
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{P}_{F}
, and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}\diamond_{m}\mathcal{P}_{F}
such that
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\preceq\mu
and
\Gamma,\mathcal{P}\vDash\varphi\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, then:

\forall\nu\in\mathcal{L}^{\llparenthesis I\rrparenthesis_{\Gamma}}\left(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket\right)^{\dagger}(\mu).\hskip 10.00002pt\exists\mathcal{Q}.\ \exists\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}\diamond_{m}\mathcal{P}_{F}.\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\preceq\nu\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\Gamma,\mathcal{Q}\vDash\psi\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil

As in many separation logics, frame preservation is built into the semantics of the triples
(
Birkedal and Yang, 2007
;
Jung et al., 2018
)
; in addition to quantifying over a probability space
\mathcal{P}
to satisfy
\varphi
, we also quantify over a probability space
\mathcal{P}_{F}
, which describes unused resources and is preserved by the program execution. As with the separating conjunction, we will omit the
m
when
m=\mathsf{s}
. In addition, since we defined probability spaces to operate over memories
\mathsf{Mem}[S]
, without
\bot
, our triples are
fault avoiding
, which is also a standard choice for separation logics
(
Yang and O’Hearn, 2002
)
. That is, if
I\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
is valid, then we know that
C
will not encounter a memory fault starting from a distribution satisfying
\varphi
.

These triples also imply almost sure termination, or
total correctness
.
We chose to pursue total correctness, as it aligns with probabilistic liveness properties that we are interested in (
e.g.,
see
Section

6.4
). In the probabilistic context, there is no single natural notion of partial correctness, and nontermination breaks parallel composition; composing a thread with a nonterminating thread alters the behavior of the first thread even without shared state.

In the remainder of this section, we will present inference rules for deriving
pcOL
triples. We write
I\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
to mean that a triple is derivable using these rules. All of the rules are sound with respect to
Definition

5.1
.

Theorem 5.2 (Soundness).

For all of the rules in
Figures

5
,
6
,
7
and
8
, if
I\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
then
I\vDash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
.

5.1.
Rules for Sequential Commands

The rules for sequential commands are given in
Figure

5
. Although the rules appear like the standard ones for Hoare-like logics
(
Hoare, 1969
)
and separation logic
(
Reynolds, 2002
;
O’Hearn
et al., 2001
)
, they rely on the properties of linearization shown in
Section

3.3
.
In
Skip
, the precondition is preserved by a no-op, and
Seq
is the standard rule for sequential composition.

The rules for if statements are split into two cases, for when the precondition implies that the true or false branch will be taken, respectively, similar to standard Outcome Logic
(
Zilberstein, 2025
;
Zilberstein et al., 2025b
)
. These rules can be combined into a single rule for analyzing both branches using the various split rules, which we will introduce in
Section

5.3
.

\hypertarget{rule:Skip}{}\frac{\;}{I\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{skip}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textsc{Skip}}\par\hypertarget{rule:Seq}{}\frac{I\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\vartheta{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\hskip 9.24994ptI\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\vartheta{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{I\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textsc{Seq}}\\
\hypertarget{rule:IfT}{}\frac{\varphi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}b\mapsto\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\hskip 9.24994ptI\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{I\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{if}~b~\texttt{then}~C_{1}~\texttt{else}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textsc{IfT}}\par\hypertarget{rule:IfF}{}\frac{\varphi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}b\mapsto\mathsf{false}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\hskip 9.24994ptI\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{I\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{if}~b~\texttt{then}~C_{1}~\texttt{else}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textsc{IfF}}\par\hypertarget{rule:Assign}{}\frac{\varphi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\mapsto E\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\land(\psi\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{own}(x)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil)}{I\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\coloneqq e~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\mapsto E\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textsc{Assign}}\par\hypertarget{rule:Samp}{}\frac{\varphi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\mapsto E\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\land(\psi\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{own}(x)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil)}{I\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\mathbin{:\approx}d(e)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi\ast(x\sim d(E){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textsc{Samp}}

Figure 5.
Rules for Sequential Commands

Finally, we give rules for atomic actions.
Assign
requires the precondition to determine that the program expression
e
evaluates to the logical expression
E
, and that the variable
x
, which is being assigned, is owned by the current thread and is disjoint from the assertion
\psi
. This structure gives the flexibility to apply the rule both when
\varphi=\psi\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\mapsto E^{\prime}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
and
\varphi=\psi\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{own}(x)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
.
Samp
has a similar requirement, but ultimately concludes that
x
is distributed according to
d(E)
rather than having a deterministic value.

5.2.
Concurrent Separation Logic Rules

Next, in
Figure

6
, we have a variety of rules inspired by Concurrent Separation Logic (
CSL
)
(
O’Hearn, 2004
;
Brookes, 2004
;
Vafeiadis, 2011
)
.
First is the
Par
rule for parallel composition. Although
Par
looks like the analogous rule from
CSL
—aside from the condition about precision—the soundness of the rule is substantially more complicated due to the probabilistic interpretation of the separating conjunction. It is not hard to imagine situations where the scheduler can introduce correlation between variables. For example, in the following program (which we previously saw in
Section

2.3
), the scheduler could choose to schedule the
y\coloneqq 1
action
after
the sampling operation is resolved, meaning that it could make
x=y
with probability 1, a clear correlation.

x\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\fatsemi y\coloneqq 0\hskip 10.00002pt{\parallel}\hskip 11.74988pty\coloneqq 1

As such, the outcomes of the two threads will not be independent after being run concurrently, but rather only
observably
independent in some restricted event space. By requiring the postconditions of each thread to be precise, we know that the probability of each measurable event must be specified exactly, so that the nondeterministic behavior of the scheduler will not be measurable (
Lemmas

C.5
and
C.6
). In the case of the program above, the strongest precise assertion about
y
is
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y\in\{0,1\}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, that
y
is always either 0 or 1, and
\left(x\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\right)\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y\in\{0,1\}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
is a valid postcondition for the program, since almost sure assertions
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
are trivially independent from all other assertions.

More formally, the soundness proof uses the fact that
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
are precise to obtain unique minimal probability spaces
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
satisfying them. We then show that for any distribution
\nu
resulting from running
C_{1}\parallel C_{2}
, and for any events
B_{1}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}
and
B_{2}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}_{2}}
, it must be the case that
\nu(B_{1}\ast B_{2})=\mu_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}(B_{1})\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{Q}_{2}}(B_{2})
. Since
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
may not terminate in a bounded amount of time, this probability only converges to the desired product in the limit.

\hypertarget{rule:Par}{}\frac{I\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\hskip 9.24994ptI\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathsf{precise}(\psi_{1},\psi_{2})}{I\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{1}\ast\varphi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}\parallel C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{1}\ast\psi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textsc{Par}}\\
\hypertarget{rule:Atom}{}\frac{J\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~a~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}I\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{{I\ast J}\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~a~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textsc{Atom}}\par\hypertarget{rule:Share}{}\frac{I\ast J\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathsf{finitary}(I)}{J\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}I\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textsc{Share}}\par\hypertarget{rule:Frame}{}\frac{I\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{I\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi\ast_{m}\vartheta{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi\ast_{m}\vartheta{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textsc{Frame}}\par\hypertarget{rule:Weaken}{}\frac{I\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{I\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textsc{Weaken}}\par\hypertarget{rule:Strengthen}{}\frac{I\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\;\;\mathsf{precise}(\psi)}{I\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textsc{Strengthen}}

Figure 6.
Concurrent Separation Logic Rules

The next two rules are for interacting with invariants. The
Atom
rule opens the invariant by moving it into the triple as an almost sure assertion, as long as the program is a single atomic action
a
. The fact that that the program executes atomically, and that
I
is true before and after execution, means that
I
is true at every step.
Next, the
Share
rule allows a finitary almost sure assertion
I
to be moved into the invariant. The soundness of this rule relies on the invariant monotonicity property that we discussed in
Section

3.3
.

Lemma 5.3 (Invariant Monotonicity).

For any
U,V,W\subseteq\mathsf{Var}
and
\sigma\in\mathsf{Mem}[W]
such that
U\cap V=\emptyset
,
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[U]
,
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[V]
, and
U\cup V\subseteq W
:

\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{I}\ast\mathcal{J}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})(\sigma)\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})(\sigma)

Recall that
\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}
is equivalent to
\supseteq
, so invariant monotonicity states that expanding the invariant (via
\ast
) can only add new behaviors to the set of outcomes.
Lemma

5.3
follows from the more general monotonicity property of linearization
(
Zilberstein
et al., 2025a
)
.

The
Frame
rule allows a local specification to be lifted into a larger memory footprint
(
Yang and O’Hearn, 2002
)
. The type of separation used depends on the mode
m
of the triple. If
m=\mathsf{s}
, then the frame
\vartheta
not only represents a disjoint set of physical
resources
, but also that those resources are distributed independently from the information about the present program. A strong triple can always be weakened to a weak triple using the
Weaken
rule. A weak triple can be strengthened—via
Strengthen
—as long as the postcondition is precise. Just as with the
Par
rule, precision here ensures that the scheduler cannot force any correlation between the postcondition and the frame.

5.3.
Structural and Outcome Splitting Rules

Additional structural rules are given in
Figure

7
.
The first four splitting rules enable pointwise reasoning over outcome conjunctions, similar to those of Demonic Outcome Logic
(
Zilberstein et al., 2025b
)
. All these rules require that the logical variable
X
, bound by the outcome conjunction, does not appear free in the invariant
I
, since
X
is unbound in the premise of the rule. If
X
is free in
I
, then the rule can be applied after
\alpha
-renaming
X
in the outcome conjunction (see
Figure

4
).

The first rule,
Split1
, requires that
\psi
dictates the partition of the probability spaces in order to construct a final direct sum. This is done in a similar fashion to rules for establishing precision that we saw in
Section

4.3
—by requiring that
\psi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\mapsto X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
for some expression
e
. Since
X
takes on distinct values in each case of the direct sum, then
e\mapsto X
witnesses that the sample space can be partitioned. If
\psi
does not witness a partition, then the
Split2
rule can instead be used, which requires
\psi
to be convex and not dependent on
X
.

\hypertarget{rule:Split1}{}\frac{I\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\hskip 9.24994pt\psi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\mapsto X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\hskip 9.24994ptX\notin\mathsf{fv}(I)}{I\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{X\sim d(E)}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{X\sim d(E)}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textsc{Split1}}\par\hypertarget{rule:NSplit1}{}\frac{I\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\hskip 9.24994pt\psi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\mapsto X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\hskip 9.24994ptX\notin\mathsf{fv}(I)}{I\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bignd_{X\in E}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bignd_{X\in E}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textsc{NSplit1}}\par\hypertarget{rule:Split2}{}\frac{I\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathsf{convex}(\psi)\hskip 9.24994ptX\notin\mathsf{fv}(I,\psi)}{I\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{X\sim d(E)}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textsc{Split2}}\par\hypertarget{rule:NSplit2}{}\frac{I\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathsf{convex}(\psi)\hskip 9.24994ptX\notin\mathsf{fv}(I,\psi)}{I\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bignd_{X\in E}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textsc{NSplit2}}\par\hypertarget{rule:Exists}{}\frac{I\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bignd_{X\in E}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\hskip 9.24994pt\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}e\mapsto X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil}{I\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\exists X\in E.\ P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textsc{Exists}}\par\hypertarget{rule:Consequence}{}\frac{\varphi^{\prime}\Rightarrow\varphi\hskip 9.24994ptI\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\hskip 9.24994pt\psi\Rightarrow\psi^{\prime}}{I\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textsc{Consequence}}

Figure 7.
Structural and Outcome Splitting Rules

We now demonstrate these two modes of use. Below,
x
is initially distributed according to some distribution
d(E)
, and then it is incremented. The sample space is still partitioned after the increment, which is witnessed by
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x-1\mapsto X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, so
Split1
can be used.

\frac{\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\mapsto X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\coloneqq x+1~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\mapsto X+1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\hskip 10.00002pt\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\mapsto X+1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x-1\mapsto X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil}{\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{X\sim d(E)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\mapsto X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\coloneqq x+1~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{X\sim d(E)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\mapsto X+1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 10.00002pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Split1}{\footnotesize{Split1}}}

On the other hand, the next program samples
y
, and the resulting sample space is not partitioned according to
X
. Instead,
Split2
is used since the postcondition is convex.

\frac{\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\mapsto X\ast X\in\{0,1\}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~y\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left({x}/2\right)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\exists Y.\ \color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Y\leq 1/2\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast y\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(Y\right){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{X\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\mapsto X\ast X\in\{0,1\}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~y\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left({x}/2\right)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\exists Y.\ \color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Y\leq 1/2\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast y\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(Y\right){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 10.00002pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Split2}{\footnotesize{Split2}}}

NSplit1
and
NSplit2
are the nondeterministic analogues of the two aforementioned rules. They operate in exactly the same way when the distribution over
X
is not known.

The
Exists
rule allows for a more complex form of case analysis, where the logical variable being scrutinized,
X
, is bound by an existential quantifier inside of a pure assertion. Since the precondition is a pure assertion, it may be satisfied by a probability space that cannot measure the probability that
X
takes on each value of
E
. Nonetheless,
Exists
allows us to use a stronger precondition where
X
is instead bound by a
\bignd
, allowing us to do case analysis using
NSplit1
or
NSplit2
. The tradeoff is that this form of reasoning is incompatible with strong frame preservation because turning a pure assertion into a nondeterministic outcome conjunction has a bad interaction with the strong separating conjunction, as shown below.

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y\in\{0,1\}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast(x\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(p\right))\centernot\Rightarrow\Big(\bignd_{Y\in\{0,1\}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}y\mapsto Y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\Big)\ast(x\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(p\right))\Rightarrow\bignd_{Y\in\{0,1\}}\Big(\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}y\mapsto Y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast(x\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(p\right))\Big)

On the left hand side, it is possible that
x
and
y
are always equal. However, the first implication—which is unsound—implies that information about
x
’s distribution can be distributed into every outcome of
y
,
i.e.,

x
is distributed according to
\mathbf{Ber}\left(p\right)

for every
value of
y
. So,
Exists
is compatible only with weak frame preservation, but it nevertheless provides an important capability to do case analysis over shared state, which we will see more concretely in
Sections

5.4
,
6.1
and
6.4
. As long as the postcondition becomes precise at some later point, the
Strengthen
rule can be used to regain strong frame preservation.
Finally, the rule of
Consequence
allows pre- and postconditions to be manipulated in the standard way. The invariant can be neither strengthened nor weakened, since doing so would break the assumptions of other threads.

5.4.
Loops and Almost Sure Termination

The final proof rule is for analyzing while loops, and proving that they
almost surely
terminate—that is, they terminate with probability 1. The
BoundedRank
rule, shown in
Figure

8
, is based on rules for sequential programs due to
McIver and Morgan (2005)
and
Zilberstein et al. (2025b)
.
It revolves around a loop invariant
\varphi
and a rank. The rank
R
must be integer-valued and bounded between
\ell
and
h
,
i.e.,

\varphi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell\leq R\leq h\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
. As long as
R>\ell
, the loop continues to iterate (
\varphi\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R>\ell\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}b\mapsto\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
) and once
R
reaches
\ell
, the loop must terminate (
\varphi\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R=\ell\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}b\mapsto\mathsf{false}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
).

The premise of
BoundedRank
guarantees that the rank strictly decreases each iteration with probability at least
p>0
. Since the rank is bounded between
\ell
and
h
, this means that from any start state, the loop is guaranteed to terminate with probability at least
p^{h-\ell}>0
, which allows us to conclude that the program must almost surely terminate by the Zero-One law of
McIver and Morgan (2005, Lemma 2.6.1)
.
Finally, since the terminating outcome
\varphi[\ell/R]
is precise, there must be a unique minimal probability space
\mathcal{Q}
that satisfies it. Similar to the soundness proof of the
Par
rule, we complete the soundness proof of the
BoundedRank
rule by showing that for all
B\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}}
and all
\nu
resulting from finite approximations of the loop,
\nu(B)
converges to
\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}(B)
.

\hypertarget{rule:BoundedRank}{}\frac{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell\leq R\leq h\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\hskip 8.19447pt\varphi\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}R=\ell\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}b\mapsto\mathsf{false}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\hskip 8.19447pt\varphi\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}R>\ell\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}b\mapsto\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\end{subarray}\hskip 9.24994ptI\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R=N>\ell\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle(\bignd_{R=\ell}^{N-1}\varphi)\oplus_{\geq p}(\bignd_{R=N}^{h}\varphi){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{subarray}{c}0<p\leq 1\hskip 7.97224ptN\notin\mathsf{vars}(\varphi)\hskip 7.97224pt\mathsf{precise}(\varphi[\ell/R])\end{subarray}}{I\vdash_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bignd_{R=\ell}^{h}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{while}~b~\texttt{do}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi[\ell/R]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\textsc{BoundedRank}}

Figure 8.
The
BoundedRank
rule for almost sure termination.

As an example of how to apply this rule, consider the example program below. The program implements a sort of random walk where
x
moves towards the origin with probability
\frac{1}{2}
, otherwise it is updated to
y
, which may be altered by a parallel thread.

\texttt{RandWalk}\hskip 10.00002pt=\hskip 10.00002pt\texttt{while}~x>0~\texttt{do}~\;\;\underbrace{b\mathbin{:\approx}\textstyle\mathbf{Ber}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\fatsemi\texttt{if}~b~\texttt{then}~x\coloneqq x-1~\texttt{else}~x\coloneqq y}_{C_{\mathsf{body}}}

As long as the value of
y
is bounded, this loop almost surely terminates, which we can prove using
BoundedRank
, subject to the resource invariant
I=y\in\{0,\ldots,5\}
.
We will sketch the proof of almost sure termination here, the full derivation is shown in
Section

F.2
.
The loop invariant is
\varphi\triangleq{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\mapsto R\ast 0\leq R\leq 5\ast\mathsf{own}(b)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil}
, essentially just stating that
x
is between 0 and 5. The rank
R
is the value of
x
, and so
R
decreases each iteration with probability at least
\frac{1}{2}
. However,
x
may also be updated to
y
, which is nondeterministic, requiring a use of the
Exists
rule to conclude that
\bignd_{R=1}^{5}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\mapsto R\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
after the command
x\coloneqq y
. Clearly
\bignd_{R=1}^{5}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\mapsto R\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
is not precise, so the resulting triple is not strongly frame preserving. Ultimately, we get the following weak triple for the loop body, which states that the rank strictly decreases with probability at least
\frac{1}{2}
.

(4)

\small y\in\{0,\ldots,5\}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\mapsto N\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{\mathsf{body}}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\big(\bignd_{R=0}^{N-1}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\mapsto R\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\big)\oplus_{\geq\frac{1}{2}}\big(\bignd_{R=N}^{5}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\mapsto R\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\big){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

At this stage, the weak triple signifies that we do not know exactly how likely the program is to terminate; we can only
bound
the probability. Indeed, the scheduler can influence the likelihood that
x
takes on particular values, and can force
x
to be correlated with other state. For example, suppose we wanted to apply the
Frame
rule with
z\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\{0,\ldots,5\}\right)
. The scheduler could choose to always make
y
and
z
equal, in which case
z\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\{0,\ldots,5\}\right)
would not be independent of the postcondition of (
4
).
However, the point of
BoundedRank
is that the scheduler
cannot
affect the probability of eventual termination. As such, the postcondition at the end of the execution is
\varphi[0/R]
, which is equivalent to the precise assertion
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\mapsto 0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, allowing us to apply
Strengthen
to get the following strong triple for the entire program.

y\in\{0,\ldots,5\}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\in\{0,\ldots,5\}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{RandWalk}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\mapsto 0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

One remarkable aspect of
pcOL
is that the triple above also implies that
RandWalk
almost surely terminates when run in parallel with any other almost surely terminating program that obeys the resource invariant
I
. We get this property for free from the
Par
rule, which guarantees that the probability of any infinite interleaving of both programs converges to 0.

This holds without any fairness assumption; our semantics guarantees that each thread almost surely terminates regardless of interference from any other threads, therefore each enabled action is almost surely scheduled within a finite amount of time. Of course, there are many programs that only almost surely terminate subject to a fair scheduler, including probabilistic consensus and synchronization protocols
(
Ben-Or, 1983
;
Lehmann and Rabin, 1981
;
Rabin, 1980
)
. As we discuss in
Section

8
, we plan to augment
pcOL
with capabilities to reason about fair termination in the future.

6.
Examples

In this section, we present four examples to demonstrate how the proof rules of
pcOL
come together into more complex derivations. Proofs are sketched here, and shown fully in
Appendix

F
.

6.1.
Entropy Mixer

There are many scenarios where several potential sources of entropy or randomness are available, which must be mixed together with the guarantee that if at least one of the sources of entropy is high quality, then the output will be at least that good. A simplified example of a such scenario is modeled in the following program, where
x_{2}
is a reliable source of entropy, but
x_{1}
is unreliable, because it is derived from
y
in a way that can be controlled adversarially by the scheduler. Despite that,
z
, which is derived from
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
is a high quality source of randomness.

\texttt{EntropyMixer}\hskip 10.00002pt\triangleq\hskip 10.00002pty\coloneqq 0\fatsemi\left(\;\;x_{1}\coloneqq y\fatsemi x_{2}\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\fatsemi z\coloneqq\xor(x_{1},x_{2})\;\;{\parallel}\;\;y\coloneqq 1\;\;\right)

We will analyze this program using the invariant
I=(y\in\{0,1\})
, and conclude in the end that
z\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)
. It is easy to see that the second thread satisfies the invariant, so we will focus on the first thread.
We first show how information about
y
can be extracted from the invariant in order to give a specification for the assignment to
x_{1}
. The use of the
Exists
rule results in a weak triple.

\footnotesize\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\;}{\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y\mapsto Y\ast\mathsf{own}(x_{1})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x_{1}\coloneqq y~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x_{1}\mapsto Y\ast y\mapsto Y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\enskip\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Assign}{\footnotesize{Assign}}}}{\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bignd_{Y\in\{0,1\}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y\mapsto Y\ast{\mathsf{own}(x_{1})}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x_{1}\coloneqq y~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bignd_{Y\in\{0,1\}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x_{1}\mapsto Y\ast y\mapsto Y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\enskip\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:NSplit1}{\footnotesize{NSplit1}}}}{\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bignd_{Y\in\{0,1\}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y\mapsto Y\ast{\mathsf{own}(x_{1})}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x_{1}\coloneqq y~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}(\textstyle\bignd_{Y\in\{0,1\}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x_{1}\mapsto Y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil)\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}y\in\{0,1\}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.24454pt\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{\footnotesize{Consequence}}}}{\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{own}(x_{1})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}y\in\{0,1\}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x_{1}\coloneqq y~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}(\textstyle\bignd_{Y\in\{0,1\}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x_{1}\mapsto Y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil)\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}y\in\{0,1\}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{Right={\hyperlink{rule:Exists}{\footnotesize{Exists}}}}}{y\in\{0,1\}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{own}(x_{1})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x_{1}\coloneqq y~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bignd_{Y\in\{0,1\}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x_{1}\mapsto Y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Atom}{\footnotesize{Atom}}}

These derivations are best read moving up from the lowermost precondition and then down from the topmost postcondition.
First,
Atom
is applied to open the invariant. Next, we use
Exists
and
NSplit1
to gain access to the value of
y
, so that we can apply the
Assign
rule. After the assignment, we use
Consequence
to weaken the information about
y
and move it outside the scope of the
\bignd
so that we can close the invariant. Now, we move on to the derivation for the remainder of the thread.

\footnotesize\frac{\frac{\frac{\vdots}{I\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{1}\mapsto Y\ast\mathsf{own}(x_{2},z)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x_{2}\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right)\fatsemi z\coloneqq\xor(x_{1},x_{2})~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bigoplus_{X\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}z\mapsto\xor(Y,X)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}}{I\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{1}\mapsto Y\ast\mathsf{own}(x_{2},z)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x_{2}\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right)\fatsemi z\coloneqq\xor(x_{1},x_{2})~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}z\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{\footnotesize{Consequence}}}}{I\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bignd_{Y\in\{0,1\}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{1}\mapsto Y\ast\mathsf{own}(x_{2},z)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x_{2}\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right)\fatsemi z\coloneqq\xor(x_{1},x_{2})~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}z\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:NSplit2}{\footnotesize{NSplit2}}}

First, we must again do case analysis on the logical variable
Y
, but this time we use
NSplit2
, which requires the postcondition to be convex and not depend on
Y
. In the premise of
NSplit2
, we we show that
z
is distributed properly regardless of the value of
Y
. For any fixed
Y
, after executing the writes to
x_{2}
and
z
, we know that
\bigoplus_{X\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z\mapsto\xor(Y,X)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
(the proof is quite mechanical, and shown in
Section

F.3
).
Since
Y
is constant, then
\xor(Y,X)
is a bijection from
\{0,1\}
to
\{0,1\}
, and we can therefore use the rule of
Consequence
to conclude that
z
is uniformly distributed. Since that postcondition is precise, we can strengthen the triple. After combining the two threads with
Par
, we get the following specification for the whole program.

\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{own}(x_{1},x_{2},y,z)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{EntropyMixer}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}z\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

6.2.
Concurrent Shuffle

\begin{array}[]{ll}\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{ConcurrentShuffle}:\\
\quad a_{1}\coloneqq[]\fatsemi a_{2}\coloneqq[]\fatsemi i\coloneqq 0\fatsemi\\
\quad\texttt{while}~i<\texttt{len}(a)~\texttt{do}~(\\
\qquad b\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\fatsemi\\
\qquad\texttt{if}~b~\texttt{then}~a_{1}\coloneqq a_{1}\mathbin{+\!+}[a[i]]~\texttt{else}~a_{2}\coloneqq a_{2}\mathbin{+\!+}[a[i]]\fatsemi\\
\qquad i\coloneqq i+1\\
\quad)\fatsemi\\
\quad\texttt{shuffle}_{1}\parallel\texttt{shuffle}_{2}\fatsemi\\
\quad a\coloneqq a_{1}\mathbin{+\!+}a_{2}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{shuffle}_{k}:\\
\quad i_{k}\coloneqq\texttt{len}(a_{k})\fatsemi\\
\quad\texttt{while}~i_{k}>1~\texttt{do}\\
\qquad j_{k}\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{unif}\left([0,\ldots,i_{k}]\right)\fatsemi\\
\qquad a_{k}\coloneqq\texttt{swap}(a_{k},i_{k},j_{k})\fatsemi\\
\qquad i_{k}\coloneqq i_{k}-1\\
\\
\\
\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 9
.
A concurrent shuffling algorithm, where
\ell_{1}\mathbin{+\!+}\ell_{2}
concatenates two lists and
\texttt{swap}(\ell,i,j)
returns
\ell
with the elements
\ell[i]
and
\ell[j]
swapped.

Bacher et al. (2015)
showed that shuffling algorithms can be made up to seven times faster through parallelization. They introduced a divide-and-conquer algorithm in which sub-arrays are shuffled concurrently and then merged. In this example, we prove the correctness of a simple concurrent shuffle algorithm using
pcOL
. The program is shown in
Figure

9
.
First, the elements in the array are randomly assigned to two buckets,
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
, the buckets are then shuffled in parallel using the standard
Fisher and Yates (1938)
algorithm, and then the two shuffled sub-lists are concatenated together. For some list
\ell
, let
\Pi(\ell)
be the set of all permutations of
\ell
. For the purposes of this example, we will presume that all lists do not contain duplicate values. The specification of the Fisher-Yates shuffle is shown below (and proven in
Section

F.4
). That is, if a list
A
is stored in
a_{k}
, then
a_{k}
will hold a uniformly chosen permutation of that list after execution of
\texttt{shuffle}_{k}
.

\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a_{k}\mapsto A\ast\mathsf{own}(i_{k},j_{k})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{shuffle}_{k}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}a_{k}\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\Pi(A)\right){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

For some list
\ell
and bit-string
x
, let
\ell[x]
be the list obtained by filtering
\ell
to only contain the indices
i
such that
x[i]=1
,
e.g.,

[1,2,3,4][1001]=[1,4]
.
After the execution of the bucketing loop, we get the postcondition

\bigoplus_{X\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\{0,1\}^{\texttt{len}(A)}\right)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a_{1}\mapsto A[X]\ast a_{2}\mapsto A[\neg X]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, where
X
is a uniformly chosen bit-string that dictates into which bucket each element of
A
is placed, and
\neg X
is bitwise logical negation. Next, to analyze the concurrent calls to
\texttt{shuffle}_{k}
, we first use
Split1
so that we can separate
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
. Using
Par
with the triple for
\texttt{shuffle}_{k}
, we have that
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
are uniformly and independently distributed permutations of the initial lists after the concurrent shuffles.

\footnotesize\frac{\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a_{1}\mapsto A[X]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{shuffle}_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}a_{1}\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\Pi(A[X])\right){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\hskip 8.50012pt\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}a_{2}\mapsto A[\neg X]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{shuffle}_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}a_{2}\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\Pi(A[\neg X])\right){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a_{1}\mapsto A[X]\ast a_{2}\mapsto A[\neg X]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{shuffle}_{1}\parallel\texttt{shuffle}_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}(a_{1}\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\Pi(A[X])\right))\ast(a_{2}\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\Pi(A[\neg X]\right)){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Par}{\footnotesize{Par}}}

Reapplying the outcome conjunction over
X
, we can rewrite the postcondition to be:

\textstyle\bigoplus_{X\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\{0,1\}^{\texttt{len}(A)}\right)}\bigoplus_{A_{1}\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\Pi(A[X])\right)}\bigoplus_{A_{2}\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\Pi(A[\neg X])\right)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a_{1}\mapsto A_{1}\ast a_{2}\mapsto A_{2}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil

Using
Split1
three times and
Assign
, we get the following postcondition after the assignment to
a
.

\textstyle\bigoplus_{X\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\{0,1\}^{\texttt{len}(A)}\right)}\bigoplus_{A_{1}\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\Pi(A[X])\right)}\bigoplus_{A_{2}\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\Pi(A[\neg X])\right)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a\mapsto A_{1}\mathbin{+\!+}A_{2}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil

We now prove the assertion above implies that
a
is a uniform permutation. Take any
\ell\in\Pi(A)
and let
n=\texttt{len}(A)
. For every
0\leq k\leq n
, there is exactly one split
X
such that
X
assigns the elements
\ell[0],\ldots,\ell[k-1]
to
a_{1}
and the rest to
a_{2}
. This split occurs with probability
\frac{1}{2^{n}}
.
Further, given that this correct split has occurred,
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
are shuffled so that
a_{1}\mathbin{+\!+}a_{2}=\ell
with probability
\frac{1}{k!}\frac{1}{(n-k)!}
. Thus the probability of getting the permutation
\ell
is
\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{1}{2^{n}}\frac{1}{k!}\frac{1}{(n-k)!}=\frac{1}{n!}
.
Since there are exactly
|\Pi(A)|=n!
permutations, this means that these permutations are produced uniformly, therefore:

\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a\mapsto A\ast\mathsf{own}(\cdots)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{ConcurrentShuffle}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}a\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\Pi(A)\right){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

6.3.
Private Information Retrieval

\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{PrivFetch}:\\
\quad q_{1}\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{unif}\left(\{0,1\}^{n}\right)\fatsemi\\
\quad q_{2}\coloneqq\xor(q_{1},x)\fatsemi\\
\quad\texttt{fetch}_{1}\parallel\texttt{fetch}_{2}\fatsemi\\
\quad r\coloneqq\xor(r_{1},r_{2})\\
\end{array}\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{fetch}_{k}:\\
\quad i_{k}\coloneqq 0\fatsemi r_{k}\coloneqq 0\fatsemi\\
\quad\texttt{while}~i_{k}<\texttt{len}(q_{k})~\texttt{do}\\
\qquad\texttt{if}~q_{k}[i_{k}]=1~\texttt{then}\\
\qquad\quad r_{k}\coloneqq\xor(r_{k},d[i_{k}])\fatsemi\\
\qquad i_{k}\coloneqq i_{k}+1\end{array}

Figure 10
.
A concurrent private information retrieval protocol.

Private information retrieval allows a user to fetch data without the database operator learning what data was requested
(
Chor
et al., 1998
)
.
A simple form of private information retrieval is modeled in the program shown in
Figure

10
. The
\texttt{fetch}_{k}
programs process a bit string query
q_{k}
, indicating which entries of the database
d
to return. Those entries are then bitwise xor’ed together.
Private retrieval is implemented in
PrivFetch
. The input
x
is a
one-hot
bit string
\texttt{onehot}(K)
, with a 1 in position
K
—indicating the index of the data to retrieve—and zeros everywhere else. Two queries are then made concurrently. The first one uses a randomly chosen bit string, and the second uses the same random string xor’ed with
\texttt{onehot}(K)
. The final data is an xor of the two responses, which reveals the data at position
K
.

Barthe
et al. (2020)
proved a similar example in
PSL
, but their version was sequential; both fetches happened within a single for loop. Our version better models a distributed system where the computation does not occur in lockstep. We first present a specification for
\texttt{fetch}_{k}
(proven in
Section

F.5
), which states that
r
is an xor of data entries
i
, such that
q_{k}[i]=1
, subject to the invariant that
d\mapsto D
.

d\mapsto D\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q_{k}\mapsto Q\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{own}(i_{k},r_{k})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{fetch}_{k}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}r_{k}\mapsto\xor_{0\leq i<n:Q[i]=1}D[i]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

We now sketch the derivation of the main procedure, the full proof is shown in
Section

F.5
. After sampling into
q_{1}
, we use
Split2
to make the outcome of that query deterministic. After assigning
q_{2}
, we get
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q_{1}\mapsto Q\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}q_{2}\mapsto\xor(Q,\texttt{onehot}(K))\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
. We can then apply the
Par
rule to analyze the concurrent fetches to get the following postcondition:

{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r_{1}\mapsto\xor_{0\leq i<n:Q[i]=1}D[i]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}r_{2}\mapsto\xor_{0\leq i<n:\xor(Q,\texttt{onehot}(K))[i]=1}D[i]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil}

The value of
r
is the xor of
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, which differ only at index
K
, therefore we can conclude that
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r\mapsto D[K]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
. Since this assertion is convex and does not depend on
Q
, we meet the side conditions of the
Cond2
rule, and therefore the final postcondition is simply
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r\mapsto D[K]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
.

\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\mapsto\texttt{onehot}(K)\ast d\mapsto D\ast\mathsf{own}(q_{1},q_{1},r_{1},r_{2},r,i_{1},i_{2})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{PrivFetch}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}r\mapsto D[K]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

6.4.
The von Neumann Trick

The
von Neumann (1951)
trick is a protocol for simulating a fair coin given a coin with unknown bias. The biased coin is flipped twice, if the outcome is 1-0 or 0-1, then the output is the first coin flip, otherwise the experiment is repeated. Below, we have a variant of the von Neumann trick where the coin’s bias is stored in shared memory, and its value is not remembered across rounds.

\texttt{vonNeumann}\hskip 10.00002pt\triangleq\hskip 10.00002ptx\coloneqq 0\fatsemi y\coloneqq 0\fatsemi\phantom{\;}\texttt{while}~x=y~\texttt{do}~\;\;p^{\prime}\coloneqq p\fatsemi x\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(p^{\prime}\right)\fatsemi y\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(p^{\prime}\right)

Every round, a concurrent thread could change the bias, altering the probability of terminating in that round. Despite this, we show that the program almost surely terminates, and that
x
is distributed according to a fair coin, subject to the invariant
I=(p\in[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]_{\Delta})
where
0<\varepsilon\leq\frac{1}{2}
is an arbitrarily small probability and
\Delta
is a nonzero step size, making the interval finite (if
p
could be 0 or 1, then the program may not terminate). A variant of this example appeared in
Zilberstein et al. (2025b)
, where the bias was explicitly altered by an adversary. This concurrent version of the program introduces new challenges from a program analysis perspective.
The bulk of the derivation involves analyzing the while loop. To do so, we need a loop invariant, which is shown below.

\varphi\triangleq\varphi_{0}\vee\varphi_{1}\hskip 10.00002pt\varphi_{0}\triangleq\bigoplus_{X\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\mapsto X\ast y\mapsto\lnot X\ast R=0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\hskip 10.00002pt\varphi_{1}\triangleq\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=y\mapsto\mathsf{true}\ast R=1\ast\mathsf{own}(p^{\prime})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil

The rank
R
is either 0 or 1. When
R=0
,
x\neq y
and
x
is uniformly distributed, so the loop terminates. When
R=1
,
x=y
, so the loop keeps iterating. Each iteration, the loop terminates with probability at least
2\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)
. Due to the reliance on shared state, this probability is not exact, but only a bound.
The structure of the proof is similar to that of
Section

6.1
. First, we use
Atom
and
NSplit1
to open the invariant and conclude that
p^{\prime}
holds some probability in
[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]_{\Delta}
. Since the postcondition is imprecise and based on shared state, we are only able to obtain a weak triple at this point.

I\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{own}(p^{\prime})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~p^{\prime}\coloneqq p~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bignd_{X\in[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]_{\Delta}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}p^{\prime}\mapsto X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

We then sequence this with the two sampling operations, shown below:

\footnotesize\hskip-17.00024pt\frac{\frac{\frac{\vdots}{I\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p^{\prime}\mapsto X\ast\mathsf{own}(x,y)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(p^{\prime}\right)\fatsemi y\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(p^{\prime}\right)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}p^{\prime}\mapsto X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast x\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(X\right)\ast y\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(X\right){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}}{I\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p^{\prime}\mapsto X\ast\mathsf{own}(x,y)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(p^{\prime}\right)\fatsemi y\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(p^{\prime}\right)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{0}\oplus_{\geq 2\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)}\varphi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{\footnotesize{Consequence}}}}{I\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bignd_{X\in[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]_{\Delta}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p^{\prime}\mapsto X\ast\mathsf{own}(x,y)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(p^{\prime}\right)\fatsemi y\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(p^{\prime}\right)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{0}\oplus_{\geq 2\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)}\varphi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:NSplit2}{\footnotesize{NSplit2}}}

We first use
NSplit2
to show that for any fixed probability
X
, the loop terminates with probability at least
2\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)
. This is done using straightforward combinatorial reasoning. Given that
p^{\prime}\mapsto X
, we know that
x
and
y
will be independently and identically distributed according to
\mathbf{Ber}\left(X\right)
. That means that
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\mapsto 1\ast y\mapsto 0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
and
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\mapsto 0\ast y\mapsto 1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
both occur with probability
X(1-X)
, so
\varphi_{0}
occurs with probability
2X(1-X)
, and otherwise
x=y
, so
\varphi_{1}
holds. Since we know that
X\in[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]
, then clearly
2\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)\leq 2X(1-X)
, so the consequence above is valid.
After applying
BoundedRank
, we get the postcondition
\varphi_{0}\Rightarrow(x\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right))
. Since this postcondition is precise, we can use
Strengthen
to get the following strong triple for the whole program, indicating both that the program almost surely terminates, and that
x
is distributed like a fair coin. In fact, it also terminates when composed in parallel with other terminating threads that alter
p
in arbitrary ways!

p\in[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]_{\Delta}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{own}(x,y,p^{\prime})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{vonNeumann}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}x\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

7.
Related Work

Logics for Probabilistic Concurrency

Polaris is a relational separation logic built on Iris
(
Jung et al., 2018
)
for reasoning about concurrent probabilistic programs
(
Tassarotti, 2018
;
Tassarotti and
Harper, 2019
)
.
Compared to Polaris,
pcOL
has two main advantages: it supports unbounded looping and it supports direct probabilistic reasoning about the distribution of outcomes. Polaris cannot handle the von Neumann trick, which involves unbounded looping. Because it is a relational logic, Polaris works by relating a randomized concurrent program to a specification program, which is randomized and nondeterministic (but not concurrent). This requires the random choices between the two programs to be coupled in lockstep.

In our concurrent shuffling example, we prove with
pcOL
that a sequence of many random choices results in a completely different uniform distribution of outcomes. It would be impossible to recreate this proof in Polaris, since the natural specification program for a randomized shuffle would only make a single random choice (a uniform sample over permutations), whereas the actual program makes a large sequence of random choices.
It might be possible to do an alternate relational proof in Polaris by instead picking a specification program that makes a similar sequence of random choices, but then one would be left with the challenge of proving that such a specification program actually generated a uniform shuffle, for which Polaris cannot help.

In
pcOL
, we combine the two steps, avoiding the need to write down a specification program by building quantitative reasoning tools into the logic itself. However, the approaches do have similarities—our invariant sensitive semantics implicitly captures the behavior of the specification program by converting parallel manipulation of shared state into nondeterminism.
This is slightly easier, since the user must only write down an invariant rather than an entire specification program.
Then, rather than externally analyzing the specification program, we directly prove a quantitative postcondition. While
pcOL
does not support all the capabilities of Polaris (
e.g.,
ghost state, higher order state, etc.), proofs are carried out in fewer steps using a single, self-contained logic.
In the future, it may be fruitful to combine the two approaches. Indeed,
Bao
et al. (2025)
showed the advantages of supporting both relational and unary reasoning in a single probabilistic logic.

Fesefeldt
et al. (2022)
pursued an alternative technique for reasoning about probabilistic concurrent programs, based on a
quantitative
interpretation of separation logic
(
Batz et al., 2019
)
.
Their logic can be used to lower bound the probability of a single outcome, making all but the private information retrieval example in this paper out of reach. While
Fesefeldt
et al.
do support unbounded looping, the probability of nontermination is always added to the final expected values, and so it cannot be used to prove almost sure termination.

Fan
et al. (2025)
developed another logic based on Probabilistic Rely-Guarantee
(
McIver
et al., 2016
)
. While it supports outcome splitting, the program must be re-instrumented to explicitly declare where splitting occurs, whereas in
pcOL
splitting is purely logical and can be used anywhere.
Fan
et al.
require postconditions after splitting to be convex; they have a rule similar to
Split2
, but not
Split1
. In addition, their parallel composition rules do not give independence guarantees, and accordingly can only make conclusions about the local distributions of all threads, and not the
joint
distributions of all the variables in global memory, and programs must be proven to almost surely terminate externally to the logic.
Their logic is based on an oblivious adversary model—a weaker model than our unrestricted adversary. More programs are verifiable in this model, so it is often preferred, however the logic of
Fan
et al.
can only reason about obliviousness in limited ways,
i.e.,
by treating coin flip actions as atomic, so many programs are still out of reach.

Probabilistic Separation Logics

Capturing probabilistic independence in separation logic was first explored by
Barthe
et al. (2020)
, however the resulting Probabilistic Separation Logic (
PSL
) was limited in its ability to reason about control flow, and the frame rule had stringent side conditions.
DIBI
later extended the
PSL
model to include conditioning
(
Bao
et al., 2021
)
. Lilac built on the two aforementioned logics and used conditioning to improve on
PSL
’s handling of control flow, although without mutable state
(
Li
et al., 2023
)
. Lilac also added support for continuous distributions, and reformulated the notion of separation using probability spaces, making it more expressive.

Lilac’s lack of mutable state means that information about variables can be duplicated; for example,
x\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\in\{0,1\}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
is satisfiable.
Bluebell uses a similar model to Lilac, with the ability to reason about mutable state, which requires the logic to track permissions too
(
Bao
et al., 2025
)
. We chose to use a more restrictive form of separation where information about variables cannot be shared, as it simplified the logical rules by eliminating the need for tracking permissions.

As we mentioned in
Section

2
, the direct sum semantics of
\bigoplus
differs from conditioning modalities
\mathsf{C}_{X\sim d}\varphi
, which are based on disintegration. However, we believe that in the future the two modalities can work together to do discrete case analysis over continuous programs. For example, consider the following program, which samples from a continuous distribution and then branches on the result.

x\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{unif}\left([0,1]\right)\fatsemi\texttt{if}~x\leq\tfrac{1}{2}~\texttt{then}~C_{1}~\texttt{else}~C_{2}

Lilac’s rule for analyzing if statements is incompatible with mutable state, so we would instead need to use
Split1
or
Split2
to do case analysis on the guard
x\leq\frac{1}{2}
in order to derive a specification for this program. However, those rules require an outcome conjunction, which cannot express the fact that
x
is distributed according to a continuous distribution. Instead, we can partition the continuous distribution into two parts, joined with an outcome conjunction, which would then allow us to analyze the two branches of the if statement.

\mathsf{C}_{X\sim\mathbf{unif}\left([0,1]\right)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\mapsto X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\hskip 10.00002pt\implies\hskip 10.00002pt\left(\mathsf{C}_{X\sim\mathbf{unif}\left([0,1/2]\right)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\mapsto X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\right)\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\mathsf{C}_{X\sim\mathbf{unif}\left((1/2,1]\right)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\mapsto X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\right)

Although the direct sum’s partitioning of the sample space imposes some limitations on the splitting rules, the benefit is that they are fully compositional; the use of splitting does not impede the application of other rules later in the derivation. This is in contrast with Bluebell’s
c-wp-swap

(
Bao
et al., 2025
, §5.1)
, which requires ownership over all program variables (denoted
\mathsf{own}_{\mathbb{X}}
), thereby precluding most later applications of the frame rule. Although
Bao
et al. (2025)
show fruitful uses of
c-wp-swap
, the restriction is not acceptable for a concurrency logic, since it would preclude use of the
Par
rule.

This is similar to
Exists
disabling strong frame preservation, but
Exists
is used in specific scenarios for case analysis on nondeterministic shared state, which only arises in the concurrency setting.
In any case, strong frame preservation can always be reenabled if the postcondition is precise.
Non-frame-preserving operations arise in non-probabilistic separation logics too
(
Vindum
et al., 2025
;
Spies et al., 2025
)
.
The idea of having two types of probabilistic separation was also explored in
LINA
, where weak separation corresponds to
negative dependence

(
Bao
et al., 2022
)
.

The notion of
precision
has been previously studied in separation logics, in part to explain when the separating conjunction distributes over other logical connectives, such as the regular conjunction
(
Calcagno
et al., 2007
;
Vafeiadis, 2011
)
.
In
PSL
, precision (under the name
supported
) was used to ensure that the guard of an if statement remains independent of the postcondition of the two branches. Conditioning à la Lilac and Bluebell provides a more flexible way to reason about control flow without forcing the guard to be independent of the states in the two branches.

Another category of probabilistic separation logics build on Iris
(
Jung et al., 2018
)
, from which they inherit expressive features, including ghost state and impredicative invariant reasoning.
In these logics, separating conjunctions have the usual meaning from
CSL
, and do not capture probabilistic independence.

Lohse and Garg (2024)
and
Haselwarter et al. (2024b)
develop logics for proving bounds on the expected runtime of a randomized program.

Aguirre et al. (2024)
