apply a similar approach for upper bounding the probability that a postcondition will fail to hold in sequential programs, and
Li et al. (2025)
extended this work to the concurrent setting.
Additional logics have also been developed for relational reasoning and refinement
(
Gregersen et al., 2024a
;
Haselwarter et al., 2024a
;
Gregersen et al., 2024b
)
.
The tradeoff is that they focus on a narrow property about programs’ probabilistic behaviors,
e.g.,
only capturing a bound on an expected cost or probability of a single event.
Outcome Separation Logic uses a more primitive form of heap separation, but is backed by a denotational model that supports specifications about the distribution of outcomes
(
Zilberstein
et al., 2024
)
.

8.
Conclusion

This paper brings together ideas from concurrent, probabilistic separation logics, and Demonic Outcome Logic in developing
pcOL
, a new expressive logic for analysis of probabilistic concurrent programs. Although
pcOL
represents a significant step in reasoning for randomized concurrent programs, more work remains to be done. Fine-grained concurrency analysis is notoriously complex, and we plan to augment
pcOL
in the following ways to support more expressive verification.

Fair Termination and Synchronization.

Our
BoundedRank
rule makes no assumptions about fairness (meaning that no thread can be indefinitely starved); indeed it applies only to programs for which the probability of eventual termination does not depend on the scheduler.
However, many probabilistic synchronization protocols
(
Hart
et al., 1983
)
such as the Dining Philosophers problem
(
Lehmann and Rabin, 1981
)
only terminate under a fair scheduler. We would like to extend
pcOL
to reason about these programs, but it will present significant challenges. Fairness is not a compositional property, so many of the properties of
\mathcal{L}(\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket)
that we rely on for soundness would not hold.

Dynamic Allocation and Resource Algebras.

As with
PSL
and all logics that build on it, our resource model uses variables rather than pointers.
However, most concurrent programs use pointers.
Modern
CSL
implementations such as Iris
(
Jung et al., 2018
;
Jung et al., 2015
)
use resource algebras, so that additional types of physical and logical state can be added to govern the ways in which concurrent threads modify shared resources. Bluebell already includes permissions, which help to duplicate knowledge about read-only variables
(
Bao
et al., 2025
)
, however many other resources are used in practice and it is not yet understood how those resources interact with the independence model of separation. We plan to augment the model of
pcOL
to support Iris style resource algebras.

Mechanization

As we saw in
Section

6
and
Appendix

F
,
pcOL
derivations are quite involved—even for small programs—due to the handling of invariants, conditioning, and case analysis on shared state. Verification of larger programs would be infeasible with pen-and-paper proofs, therefore we plan to mechanize
pcOL
in the Lean proof assistant
(
de Moura et al., 2015
)
. Lean is the ideal choice because much the underlying probability theory, domain theory, and topology needed to support
pcOL
are already formalized in mathlib
(
mathlib Community, 2020
)
, although more work would still be needed to formalize all the foundational theories of
pcOL
, such as the convex powerdomain.

Appendix

Appendix A
Definition of Program Semantics

In this section, we give details on the
Pomsets with Formulae
semantic model due to
Zilberstein
et al. (2025a)
.

A.1.
Pomsets with Formulae

Whereas standard pomsets (partially ordered multisets) use a partial order to record the causality between atomic actions in the program (elements of a multiset)
(
Gischer, 1988
;
Pratt, 1986
)
, pomsets with formulae add a Boolean formula to each node, which records which tests must succeed or fail to reach that point in the execution. This is necessary to capture probabilistic concurrency, since tests may not always pass or fail, but rather they may only have some probability of passing, and so both paths must be represented in a single semantic structure.
We briefly introduce pomsets with formulae here, refer to
Zilberstein
et al. (2025a)
for a more complete treatment.

Let
\mathsk{label}\triangleq\mathsf{Act}\cup\mathsf{Test}\cup\{\bullet,\bot\}
, which can be either an action or test (from
Figure

1
), a no-op (
\bullet
), or undetermined (
\bot
). Although
\bot
labels will never appear in the denotation of a
complete
program, they are used to indicate that a finite structure is an approximation of an infinite one,
i.e.,
in the semantics of while loops. Also, let
\mathsk{nodes}
be a countable universe of identifiers and
\mathsk{form}
be the set of Boolean formulae over
\mathsk{nodes}
. For some
\psi\in\mathsk{form}
, we write
\mathsf{sat}(\psi)
to indicate that
\psi
is satisfiable, and
\mathsf{vars}(\psi)\subseteq\mathsk{nodes}
is the set of variables referenced in
\psi
. We now define the underlying structure.

Definition A.1 (Labelled Partial Order with Formulae (LPOFs)).

An LPOF is a 4-tuple
\langle N,<,\lambda,\varphi\rangle\in{\mathsk{lpo}}
where:

(1)

N\subseteq\mathsk{nodes}
is a countable set of nodes;

(2)

\langle N,\mathord{<}\rangle
is a strict poset with a single minimal element such that finitely many nodes appear at every finite distance from the root;

(3)

\lambda\colon N\to\mathsk{label}
is a labelling function such that
x
has no successors whenever
\lambda(x)=\bot
;

(4)

\varphi\colon N\to\mathsk{form}
is a formula function such that:
\varphi(y)\Rightarrow\varphi(x)
, for all
x<y
and for all
x\in N
,
\mathsf{sat}(\varphi(x))
and
y<x
for all
y\in\mathsf{vars}(\varphi(x))
.

For some
\alpha\in{\mathsk{lpo}}
, we will often use
N_{\alpha}
,
<_{\alpha}
,
\lambda_{\alpha}
, and
\varphi_{\alpha}
to refer to its parts.

The first three components are standard in pomset semantics. The order denotes
causality
, so
x<y
means that the action
\lambda(x)
must be scheduled before
\lambda(y)
. The order is partial (not total) because actions that occur in parallel are not related. Formulae are a new addition to this structure, allowing us to encode guarded branching in the structure too. For any node
x\in N
,
\varphi(x)
is a satisfiable formula comprised of nodes appearing earlier in the trace, since the corresponding tests must be resolved before executing later actions. In addition, formulae can only become stronger as the trace goes on, since dependencies on more and more tests are accumulated over time.

Two LPOFs are isomorphic, denoted
\alpha\equiv\beta
iff there is a bijection
f\colon N_{\alpha}\to N_{\beta}
such that
x<_{\alpha}y
iff
f(x)<_{\beta}f(y)
,
\lambda_{\alpha}=\lambda_{\beta}\circ f
, and
\varphi_{\alpha}=f^{-1}\circ\varphi_{\beta}\circ f
, where
f^{-1}(\psi)
renames the variables of
\psi
in the obvious way. Given that, a pomset with formulae is an equivalence class of LPOFs.

Definition A.2 (Pomsets with Formulae).

We denote the isomorphism class of
\alpha
as
[\alpha]\triangleq\{\beta\in{\mathsk{lpo}}\mid\alpha\equiv\beta\}
.
A pomset with formulae (or, simply, a pomset)
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in{\mathsk{pom}}
is an isomorphism class of LPOFs.

{\mathsk{pom}}\triangleq\{[\alpha]\mid\alpha\in{\mathsk{lpo}}\}

Pomsets with formulae have many nice domain-theoretic properties. For example, they have a DCPO (directed complete partial order) structure, meaning that Scott continuous operations over them have least fixed points. Roughly speaking
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\beta}
iff
\boldsymbol{\beta}
is obtained by replacing nodes labelled
\bot
in
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
with a new, larger structure. We will see a concrete example of this shortly when we discuss the semantics of loops. Infinite pomsets with formulae can also be represented as the supremum of their finite approximation, and monotone operations on those finite approximations can be extended to continuous operations on infinite pomsets. This is useful, since it is not possible to define operations inductively on infinite structures, as we will see in
Section

A.2
.

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\texttt{skip}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\triangleq\boldsymbol{\langle}\bullet\boldsymbol{\rangle}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2}}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket\fatsemi\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\left\llbracket C_{1}\parallel C_{2}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket\parallel\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\texttt{if}~b~\texttt{then}~C_{1}~\texttt{else}~C_{2}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\triangleq\textsf{guard}(b,\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket)

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\texttt{while}~b~\texttt{do}~C\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{lfp}\left(\Phi_{\langle b,C\rangle}\right)\hskip 8.50012pt\text{where}\ \Phi_{\langle b,C\rangle}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\triangleq\mathsf{guard}\left(b,\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket\fatsemi\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\langle}\bullet\boldsymbol{\rangle}\right)

\displaystyle\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\triangleq\boldsymbol{\langle}a\boldsymbol{\rangle}

Figure 11
.
Trace semantics for commands
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket\colon\mathsf{Cmd}\to{\mathsk{pom}}
.

The trace semantics
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket\colon\mathsf{Cmd}\to{\mathsk{pom}}
, shown in
Figure

11
, interprets every command as a pomset with formulae. We use several operators to construct pomsets. First,
\boldsymbol{\langle}-\boldsymbol{\rangle}\colon\mathsk{label}\to{\mathsk{pom}}
constructs a singleton pomset with the given label. This is used for the semantics of
skip
and actions
a\in\mathsf{Act}
. Next, we have three combinators for combining pomsets, which we demonstrate pictorially below. The first is sequential composition
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\fatsemi\boldsymbol{\beta}
, which constructs a pomset where all the actions from
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
occur before those in
\boldsymbol{\beta}
. In the diagram,
a_{1}\to a_{2}
indicates causality (
a_{1}
occurs before
a_{2})

The second combinator is guarded choice
\mathsf{guard}(b,\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta})
, where the test
b
becomes the new root and all formulae of the nodes in
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}
are updated to indicate that the new root must pass or fail, respectively, as indicated by the arrows labelled
\mathsf{T}
and
\mathsf{F}
. Finally, parallel composition
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\parallel\boldsymbol{\beta}
joins the two pomsets with a new no-op root, so that the actions of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}
can be interleaved without restriction.

The semantics of loops is given by a least fixed point of the characteristic function
\Phi_{\langle b,C\rangle}
, which is Scott continuous since it is defined using

and

, both of which are Scott continuous
(

et al., 2025a
)
.

, as shown in the following diagram.

14.70035pt\raise-31.9419pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.38889pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.70004pt\raise-26.40936pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.84457pt\raise-26.1643pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.41365pt\raise-31.94162pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.38889pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{F}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 38.09506pt\raise-26.36115pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 37.94965pt\raise-26.11664pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces}}}}}}\\
\Phi_{\langle a,b\rangle}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\bot\boldsymbol{\rangle})&&\Phi_{\langle a,b\rangle}^{1}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\bot\boldsymbol{\rangle})&&\Phi_{\langle a,b\rangle}^{2}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\bot\boldsymbol{\rangle})&&\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\Phi_{\langle a,b\rangle}^{n}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\bot\boldsymbol{\rangle})\end{array}

In each unrolling,
\bot
appears further and further from the root until, in the supremum, it is pushed infinitely far away. That infinite spine represents the execution in which the guard
b
never becomes false. In the context of probabilistic programs, it is important to include the infinite spine, as the probability of continuing to execute may only converge to 0 in the limit.

A.2.
Linearization

The benefit of the pomset model is that it is fully compositional; the parallel composition of two commands is interpreted as a straightforward combination of their syntactic structures. However, for the purposes of designing a program logic, we need a
state transformer
model, which maps each input state to a convex set of distributions over output states. For this purpose,
Zilberstein
et al. (2025a)
also define linearization
\mathcal{L}\colon{\mathsk{pom}}\to\mathsf{Mem}[S]\to\mathcal{C}(\mathsf{Mem}[S])
. The definition is repeated below.

\displaystyle\mathsf{next}(\alpha,\psi,S)

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in N_{\alpha}\setminus S\mid\downarrow{x}\subseteq S,\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\right\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{next}^{\star}(\alpha,\psi,S)

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in N_{\alpha}\setminus S\mid\mathsf{sat}(\psi\land\varphi_{\alpha}(x))\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi,S,x)(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi,S\cup\{x\})^{\dagger}\left(\left\llbracket\lambda_{\alpha}(x)\right\rrbracket^{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathsf{Act}}(\sigma)\right)&\text{if}~\lambda_{\alpha}(x)\in\mathsf{Act}\\
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi\land\llparenthesis x=\left\llbracket\lambda_{\alpha}(x)\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Test}}(\sigma)\rrparenthesis,S\cup\{x\})(\sigma)&\text{if}~\lambda_{\alpha}(x)\in\mathsf{Test}\\
\bot_{\mathcal{C}}&\text{if}~\lambda_{\alpha}(x)=\bot\\
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi,S\cup\{x\})(\sigma)&\text{if}~\lambda_{\alpha}(x)=\bullet\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi,S)(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\eta(\sigma)&\text{if}~\mathsf{next}(\alpha,\psi,S)=\emptyset\\
\bignd_{x\in\mathsf{next}(\alpha,\psi,S)}\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi,S,x)(\sigma)&\text{if}~\mathsf{next}(\alpha,\psi,S)\neq\emptyset\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}^{\mathcal{I}}([\alpha])

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\mathsf{true},\emptyset)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\ll\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime})

The function
\mathsf{next}(\alpha,\psi,S)
gives the set of nodes that are ready to be scheduled, given a finite LPOF
\alpha
, a path condition
\psi
, and the set of nodes
S
that have already been processed. Linearizing a node
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}
does case analysis on the label of the node to decide how to proceed. If the node is an action, then the action is evaluated and composed with the linearization of the remainder of the LPOF. If the node is a test, then the result of the test is added to the path condition. If the node is
\bot
, then the execution is halted. If it is a no-op, then the execution simply continues.

To linearize an entire finite LPOF, we simply perform a nondeterministic choice over all the next nodes, linearize those nodes, and then proceed processing the rest of the structure recursively. To make the recursion well-founded, the structure must be finite, but we extend to the infinite case later. To linearize a finite pomset
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, we simply linearize any representative LPOF
\alpha\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}
. Finally, to linearize an infinite pomset, we take the supremum over the linearization of all the finite approximations, where
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\ll\boldsymbol{\alpha}
indicates that
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}
is a finite approximation of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
.

Since most operations are continuous, we get,
e.g.,
that the supremum of finite approximations of a sequential composition is equal to the sequential composition over the supremum of finite approximations:

\sup_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\ll\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}\ll\boldsymbol{\beta}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\fatsemi\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}=\left(\sup_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\ll\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\right)\fatsemi\left(\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}\ll\boldsymbol{\beta}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}\right)=\boldsymbol{\alpha}\fatsemi\boldsymbol{\beta}

The exception to this is for parallel composition, which is not continuous for technical reasons. However, if we define
\ll_{1}
as follows:

\boldsymbol{\gamma}\ll_{1}\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\prime}\hskip 17.00024pt\text{iff}\hskip 17.00024pt\boldsymbol{\gamma}\ll\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\prime}\hskip 8.50012pt\text{and}\hskip 8.50012pt\mathsf{root}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\mathsf{root}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\prime})=\bullet\hskip 8.50012pt\text{or}\hskip 8.50012pt\mathsf{root}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\prime})\neq\bullet

where
\mathsf{root}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})
is the label of the root node of the pomset
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
, then we get the following weaker pseudo-continuity property:

\sup_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\ll_{1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}\ll_{1}\boldsymbol{\beta}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\parallel\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}=\left(\sup_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\ll_{1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\right)\parallel\left(\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}\ll_{1}\boldsymbol{\beta}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}\right)=\boldsymbol{\alpha}\parallel\boldsymbol{\beta}

Appendix B
General Lemmas

B.1.
Measure Theory Lemmas

We begin by proving some general properties for characterizing discrete product spaces. By Lemma C.1 and C.2 of
Bao
et al. (2025)
, for any discrete probability space
\mathcal{P}
, there exists a countable partition
\{A_{i}\mid i\in I\}
of
\Omega_{\mathcal{P}}
such that
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}}=\{\biguplus_{i\in I^{\prime}}A_{i}\mid I^{\prime}\subseteq I\}
. Let
\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{P})
denote this partition. It follows that for every
A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}}
,
\mu_{\mathcal{P}}(A)=\sum_{B\in S}\mu_{\mathcal{P}}(B)
for some
S\subseteq\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{P})
.

Lemma B.1.

For any probability spaces
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}

\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{2}}=\left\{\biguplus_{(A,B)\in S}A\ast B~\middle|~S\subseteq\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{P}_{2})\right\}

Proof.

The forward inclusion follows immediately from Lemma C.5 of
Bao
et al. (2025)
, so it suffices to only show the reverse inclusion. Take any
S\subseteq\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{P}_{2})
.
For each
(A,B)\in S\subseteq\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{P}_{2})
, clearly
A\in\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{P}_{1})
and
B\in\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{P}_{2})
, so
A\ast B\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{2}}
since by definition
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{2}}
is the smallest sigma algebra containing
\{A\ast B\mid A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}},B\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}\}
. Since
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{2}}
is closed under countable unions, then
\biguplus_{(A,B)\in S}A\ast B\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{2}}
.
∎

Lemma B.2.

\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{P}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{2}
iff:

(1)

\Omega_{\mathcal{Q}}=\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}\ast\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}

(2)

\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}}=\{\biguplus_{(A,B)\in S}A\ast B\mid S\subseteq\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{P}_{2})\}

(3)

\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}(A\ast B)=\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}(A)\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}(B)
for all
A\in\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{P}_{1})
and
B\in\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{P}_{2})

Proof.

The forward direction follows immediately from the definition of product spaces and
Lemma

B.1
. We now show the reverse direction. By (1) and the definition of product spaces,
\Omega_{\mathcal{Q}}=\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}\ast\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}=\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. By (2) and
Lemma

B.1
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}}=\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. Now, take any event
A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}}=\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. We already know that
A=\biguplus_{(A_{1},A_{2})\in S}A_{1}\ast A_{2}
for some
S\subseteq\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{P}_{2})
, so:

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}(A)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}\left(\biguplus_{(A_{1},A_{2})\in S}A_{1}\ast A_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(A_{1},A_{2})\in S}\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}(A_{1}\ast A_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(A_{1},A_{2})\in S}\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}(A_{1})\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}(A_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(A_{1},A_{2})\in S}\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{2}}(A_{1}\ast A_{2})

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{2}}\left(\biguplus_{(A_{1},A_{2})\in S}A_{1}\ast A_{2}\right)\ =\ \mu_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{2}}(A)

∎

Lemma B.3.

If both sides of the following equality are well-defined, then:

\left(\bigoplus_{i\sim\nu}\mathcal{P}_{i}\right)\otimes\mathcal{Q}=\bigoplus_{i\sim\nu}(\mathcal{P}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{Q})

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{i}=\langle\Omega_{i},\mathcal{F}_{i},\mu_{i}\rangle
for each
i\in I=\mathsf{supp}(\nu)
. If the above are well defined, then there must be
S
and
T
such that
\Omega_{i}\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[S]
and
\Omega_{\mathcal{Q}}\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[T]
.
We first show that both probability spaces have the same sample space, since:

\displaystyle\Omega_{(\bigoplus_{i\sim\nu}\mathcal{P}_{i})\otimes\mathcal{Q}}

\displaystyle=(\bigcup_{i\in\mathsf{supp}(\nu)}\Omega_{i})\ast\Omega_{\mathcal{Q}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\sigma\uplus\sigma^{\prime}~\middle|~\sigma\in\bigcup_{i\in I}\Omega_{i}v,\sigma^{\prime}\in\Omega_{\mathcal{Q}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i\in I}\{\sigma\uplus\sigma^{\prime}\mid\sigma\in\Omega_{i},\sigma^{\prime}\in\Omega_{\mathcal{Q}}\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i\in I}\Omega_{i}\ast\Omega_{\mathcal{Q}}\ =\ \Omega_{\bigoplus_{i\sim\nu}(\mathcal{P}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{Q})}

To show that the
\sigma
-algebras are the same, it will suffice to show that they are generated from the same disjoint partitions
(
Bao
et al., 2025
, Lemma C.1)
. We start by using
Lemma

B.1
to conclude that:

\displaystyle\mathsf{ev}\left(\left(\bigoplus_{i\sim\nu}\mathcal{P}_{i}\right)\otimes\mathcal{Q}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{ev}\left(\bigoplus_{i\sim\nu}\mathcal{P}_{i}\right)\times\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{Q})

Since all the
\mathcal{P}_{i}
have disjoint sample spaces, their partitions must also be disjoint:

\displaystyle=\left(\biguplus_{i\in I}\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{P}_{i})\right)\times\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{Q})

\displaystyle=\biguplus_{i\in I}\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{P}_{i})\times\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{Q})

\displaystyle=\biguplus_{i\in I}\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{P}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{Q})=\mathsf{ev}\left(\bigoplus_{i\sim\nu}\mathcal{P}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{Q}\right)

Finally, by
Lemma

B.2
, it suffices to show that the product measures agree on events of the form
A\ast B
, where
A\in\mathcal{F}_{\bigoplus_{i\sim\nu}}\mathcal{P}_{i}
and
B\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}}
:

\displaystyle\mu_{(\bigoplus_{i\sim\nu}\mathcal{P}_{i})\otimes\mathcal{Q}}(A\ast B)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\bigoplus_{i\sim\nu}\mathcal{P}_{i}}(A)\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}(B)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i\in I}\nu(i)\cdot\mu_{i}(A\cap\Omega_{i})\right)\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}(B)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I}\nu(i)\cdot\mu_{i}(A\cap\Omega_{i})\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}(B)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I}\nu(i)\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{Q}}((A\cap\Omega_{i})\ast B)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I}\nu(i)\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{Q}}((A\ast B)\cap(\Omega_{i}\ast\Omega_{\mathcal{Q}}))

\displaystyle=\mu_{\bigoplus_{i\sim\nu}\mathcal{P}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{Q}}(A\ast B)

∎

Lemma B.4 (Monotonicity of
\otimes
).

If
\mathcal{P}\preceq\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{Q}\preceq\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
, then
\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{Q}\preceq\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
S
,
S^{\prime}
,
T
, and
T^{\prime}
be sets such that
\Omega_{\mathcal{P}}\subseteq S
,
\Omega_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[S^{\prime}]
,
\Omega_{\mathcal{Q}}\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[T]
, and
\Omega_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[T^{\prime}]
.
First we show the condition on sample spaces:

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{Q}}

\displaystyle=\Omega_{\mathcal{P}}\ast\Omega_{\mathcal{Q}}

\displaystyle=\{\sigma\uplus\tau\mid\sigma\in\Omega_{\mathcal{P}},\tau\in\Omega_{\mathcal{Q}}\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\{\sigma\uplus\tau\mid\sigma\in\pi_{S}(\Omega_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}),\tau\in\pi_{T}(\Omega_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}})\}

\displaystyle=\{\pi_{S}(\sigma)\uplus\pi_{T}(\tau)\mid\sigma\in\Omega_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}},\tau\in\Omega_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\}

\displaystyle=\{\pi_{S\cup T}(\sigma\uplus\tau)\mid\sigma\in\Omega_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}},\tau\in\Omega_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\}=\pi_{S\cup T}(\Omega_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{Q}})

Now we show the condition on
\sigma
-algebras:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{Q}}

\displaystyle=\sigma(\{A\ast B\mid A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}},B\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}}\})

Since
\sigma
-closure is monotonic:

\displaystyle\subseteq\sigma(\{A\ast B\mid A\in\pi_{S}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}),B\in\pi_{T}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}})\})

\displaystyle=\sigma(\{\pi_{S}(A)\ast\pi_{T}(B)\mid A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}},B\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\})

\displaystyle=\sigma(\pi_{S\cup T}(\{A\ast B\mid A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}},B\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\}))

\displaystyle=\pi_{S\cup T}(\sigma(\{A\ast B\mid A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}},B\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\}))

\displaystyle=\pi_{S\cup T}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}})

Finally, by
Lemma

B.2
, it suffices to show that the product measure is equal only for events of the form
A\ast B
where
A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}}
and
B\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}}
:

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{Q}}(A\ast B)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathcal{P}}(A)\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}(B)

Since
\mathcal{P}\preceq\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{Q}\preceq\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\left(\bigcup\{A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\mid\pi_{S}(A^{\prime})=A\}\right)\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\left(\bigcup\{B^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\mid\pi_{T}(B^{\prime})=B\}\right)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\left(\left(\bigcup\{A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\mid\pi_{S}(A^{\prime})=A\}\right)\ast\left(\bigcup\{B^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\mid\pi_{T}(B^{\prime})=B\}\right)\right)

Let
A^{\prime\prime}=\bigcup\{A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\mid\pi_{S}(A^{\prime})=A\}
and
B^{\prime\prime}=\bigcup\{B^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\mid\pi_{T}(B^{\prime})=B\}
. Clearly
A^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
and
B^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}
since both sets are closed under countable unions. Therefore:

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\left(A^{\prime\prime}\ast B^{\prime\prime}\right)

We show that the sets above and below are equal by showing the inclusion in both directions. The forward inclusion is trivial;
A^{\prime\prime}\ast B^{\prime\prime}
is clearly an element of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}
and has the property
\pi_{S\cup T}(A^{\prime\prime}\ast B^{\prime\prime})=\pi_{S}(A^{\prime\prime})\ast\pi_{T}(B^{\prime\prime})=A\ast B
.
For the reverse inclusion, take some element of the set below, which must have the form
\sigma\uplus\tau
where
\sigma\in\Omega_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
and
\tau\in\Omega_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}
and
\sigma\uplus\tau\in E
for some
E\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}
such that
\pi_{S\cup T}(E)=A\ast B
. By
Lemma

B.1
,
E=\biguplus_{(E_{1},E_{2})\in S}E_{1}\ast E_{2}
for some
S\subseteq\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})\times\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})
, so
\sigma\in E_{1}
and
\tau\in E_{2}
for some
(E_{1},E_{2})\in S
. Since
\pi_{S\cup T}(E)=A\ast B
, then
\pi_{S}(E)=\biguplus_{(E_{1},-)\in S}E_{1}=A
and
\pi_{T}(E)=\biguplus_{(-,E_{2})\in S}E_{2}=B
, therefore
\sigma\in E_{1}\subseteq\pi_{S}(E)\subseteq A^{\prime\prime}
and
\tau\in E_{2}\subseteq\pi_{T}(E)\subseteq B^{\prime\prime}
, so
\sigma\uplus\tau\in A^{\prime\prime}\ast B^{\prime\prime}
.

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\left(\bigcup\{E\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\mid\pi_{S\cup T}(E)=A\ast B\}\right)

∎

Lemma B.5 (Monotonicity of
\oplus
).

If
\mathcal{P}_{v}\preceq\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{v}
for each
v\in\mathsf{supp}(\nu)
, then
\bigoplus_{v\sim\nu}\mathcal{P}_{v}\preceq\bigoplus_{v\sim\nu}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{v}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\bigoplus_{v\sim\nu}\mathcal{P}_{v}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{v\sim\nu}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{v}
, so we need to prove that
\mathcal{P}\preceq\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
. Also, let
S
and
S^{\prime}
be sets such that
\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{v}}\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[S]
and
\Omega_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{v}}\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[S^{\prime}]
for all
v
. We first establish the required property on the sample space:

\Omega_{\mathcal{P}}=\bigcup_{v\in\mathsf{supp}(\nu)}\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{v}}\subseteq\bigcup_{v\in\mathsf{supp}(\nu)}\pi_{S}(\Omega_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{v}})=\pi_{S}(\Omega_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}})

Now, we establish the property on
\sigma
-algebras.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}}

\displaystyle=\{A\mid A\subseteq\Omega_{\mathcal{P}},\forall v.\ A\cap\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{v}}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}_{v}}\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\{A\mid A\subseteq\pi_{S}(\Omega_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}),\forall v.\ A\cap\pi_{S}(\Omega_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{v}})\in\{\pi_{S}(B)\mid B\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{v}}\}\}

\displaystyle=\{\pi_{S}(A)\mid A\subseteq\Omega_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}},\forall v.\ A\cap\Omega_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{v}}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{v}}\}

\displaystyle=\{\pi_{S}(A)\mid A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\}

Finally, we establish the condition on probability measures:

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{P}}(A)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in\mathsf{supp}(\nu)}\nu(v)\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{v}}(A\cap\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{v}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in\mathsf{supp}(\nu)}\nu(v)\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{v}}\left(\bigcup\{B\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{v}}\mid\pi_{S}(B)=A\cap\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{v}}\}\right)

Since
A\subseteq\Omega_{\mathcal{P}}\subseteq\pi_{S}(\Omega_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}})
, then, we can move the intersection out of the set limits:

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in\mathsf{supp}(\nu)}\nu(v)\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{v}}\left(\left(\bigcup\{B\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\mid\pi_{S}(B)=A\}\right)\cap\Omega_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{v}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\left(\bigcup\{B\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\mid\pi_{S}(B)=A\}\right)

∎

Lemma B.6.

For any complete probability spaces
\mathcal{P}
,
(\mathcal{P}_{i})_{i\in I}
, and
\mu\in\mathcal{D}(I)
such that
\Omega_{\mathcal{P}}\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[S]
, each
\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{i}}\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[T]
, and
\bigcup_{i\in I}\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{i}}=\mathsf{Mem}[T]
,
if
\mathcal{P}\preceq\mathsf{ext}(\mathcal{P}_{i})
for all
i\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)
, then
\mathcal{P}\preceq\bigoplus_{i\sim\mu}\mathcal{P}_{i}

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{Q}=\bigoplus_{i\sim\mu}\mathcal{P}_{i}
.
Since
\mathcal{P}\preceq\mathcal{P}_{i}
, then it must be that
S\subseteq T
.
The property on sample spaces is simple:

\Omega_{\mathcal{P}}=\bigcup_{i\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\Omega_{\mathcal{P}}\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\pi_{S}(\Omega_{\mathsf{ext}(\mathcal{P}_{i})})=\bigcup_{i\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\pi_{S}(\mathsf{Mem}[T])=\pi_{S}\left(\bigcup_{i\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mathsf{Mem}[T]\right)=\pi_{S}(\Omega_{\mathcal{Q}})

Next, we verify the property on
\sigma
-algebras.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}}

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}}

\displaystyle\subseteq\bigcap_{i\in I}\{\pi_{S}(A)\mid A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{ext}(\mathcal{P}_{i})}\}

\displaystyle=\{\pi_{S}(A)\mid\forall i\in I.\ A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{ext}(\mathcal{P}_{i})}\}

Note that the completion adds information about events outside of
\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{i}}
, so if
A
is in all of the
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{ext}(\mathcal{P}_{i})}
sets, then its projection into each
\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{i}}
must be in
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}_{i}}
.

\displaystyle=\{\pi_{S}(A)\mid A\subseteq\Omega_{Q},\forall i.\ A\cap\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{i}}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}_{i}}\}

\displaystyle=\{\pi_{S}(A)\mid A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}}\}

Finally, we show the property on probability measures.

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{P}}(A)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(i)\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{P}}(A)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(i)\cdot\mu_{\mathsf{ext}(\mathcal{P}_{i})}\left(\bigcup\{B\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{ext}(\mathcal{P}_{i})}\mid\pi_{S}(B)=A\}\right)

The completion assigns zero probability to events outside of
\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{i}}
, so removing the completion and projecting into
\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{i}}
will yield the same value.

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(i)\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{i}}\left(\left(\bigcup\{B\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}}\mid\pi_{S}(B)=A\}\right)\cap\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}\left(\bigcup\{B\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}}\mid\pi_{S}(B)=A\}\right)

∎

Lemma B.7.

If
\mathcal{P}\preceq\mathsf{ext}(\mathcal{P}_{i})
for all
i\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)
, then
\mathcal{P}\preceq\bigoplus_{i\sim\mu}\mathcal{P}_{i}
.

Proof.

Let
S
be the set such that
\Omega_{\mathcal{P}}=\mathsf{Mem}[S]
,
T
be such that
\mathsf{Mem}[T]=\bigcup_{i\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{i}}
, and
\mathcal{Q}=\preceq\bigoplus_{i\sim\mu}\mathcal{P}_{i}
.
The condition on sample spaces is simple, since
\Omega_{\mathcal{P}}=\mathsf{Mem}[S]
and
\Omega_{\mathcal{Q}}=\mathsf{Mem}[T]
, and clearly
\mathsf{Mem}[S]=\pi_{S}(\mathsf{Mem}[T])
. For the condition on
\sigma
-algebras, we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}}

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{i\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}}

\displaystyle\subseteq\bigcap_{i\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\{\pi_{S}(A)\mid A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{ext}(\mathcal{P}_{i})}\}

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{i\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\{\pi_{S}(A\cup B)\mid A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}_{i}},B\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[T]\setminus\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{i}}\}

Since for each
i
in the intersection above, we can measure every sample outside of
\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{i}}
, then after taking the intersection we are only able to measure samples from
\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{i}}
according to the information provided by
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}_{i}}
, which provides the
least
information about those samples.

\displaystyle=\{\pi_{S}(A)\mid A\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[T],\forall i.\ A\cap\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{i}}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}_{i}}\}

\displaystyle=\{\pi_{S}(A)\mid A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}}\}

Now, we show the condition on probability measures:

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{P}}(A)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(i)\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{P}}(A)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(i)\cdot\mu_{\mathsf{ext}(\mathcal{P}_{i})}\left(\bigcup\{B\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{ext}(\mathcal{P}_{i})}\mid\pi_{S}(B)=A\}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(i)\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{i}}\left(\left(\bigcup\{B\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}}\mid\pi_{S}(B)=A\}\right)\cap\Omega_{\mathcal{P}_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}\left(\bigcup\{B\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}}\mid\pi_{S}(B)=A\}\right)

∎

B.2.
The Convex Powerset

Lemma B.8.

For any finite index set
I
,
f\colon X\to\mathcal{C}(Y)
, and
(S_{i})_{i\in I}\in\mathcal{C}(X)^{I}
:

f^{\dagger}(\bignd_{i\in I}S_{i})=\bignd_{i\in I}f^{\dagger}(S_{i})

Proof.

Let
g\colon I\to\mathcal{C}(X)
be defined as
g(i)\triangleq S_{i}
. Now, observe that:

\bignd_{i\in I}S_{i}=\left\{\sum_{i\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mid\mu\in\mathcal{D}(I),\forall i.\ \nu_{i}\in g(i)\right\}=g^{\dagger}(\mathcal{D}(I))

So, we get:

\displaystyle f^{\dagger}(\bignd_{i\in I}S_{i})

\displaystyle=f^{\dagger}(g^{\dagger}(\mathcal{D}(I)))

\displaystyle=(f^{\dagger}\circ g)^{\dagger}(\mathcal{D}(I))

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum_{i\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(i)\cdot\nu_{i}\;\;\Big|\;\;\mu\in\mathcal{D}(I),\forall i.\ \nu_{i}\in f^{\dagger}(g(i))\right\}

\displaystyle=\bignd_{i\in I}f^{\dagger}(S_{i})

∎

For any
S\in\mathcal{C}(\mathsf{Mem}[V])
and
A\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[V]
, let:

\mathsf{minProb}(S,A)\triangleq\inf_{\mu\in S}\mu(A)

Since
S\in\mathcal{C}(\mathsf{Mem}[V])
, it is a closed subset of
\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{Mem}[V]_{\bot})
, and so there must be a
\mu\in S
such that
\mu(A)=\mathsf{minProb}(S,A)
, therefore
\mathsf{minProb}(S,A)=\min_{\mu\in S}\mu(A)
.

Lemma B.9 (Monotonicity of
\mathsf{minProb}
).

If
S\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}T
, then:

\mathsf{minProb}(S,A)\leq\mathsf{minProb}(T,A)

Proof.

By definition
S\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}T
iff
S\supseteq T
. So, we get:

\mathsf{minProb}(S,A)=\inf_{\mu\in S}\mu(A)=\min(\inf_{\mu\in T}\mu(A),\inf_{\mu\in S\setminus T}\mu(A))\leq\inf_{\mu\in T}\mu(A)=\mathsf{minProb}(T,A)

∎

Lemma B.10.

For any directed set
D\subseteq\mathcal{C}(\mathsf{Mem}[V])
:

\sup_{S\in D}\mathsf{minProb}(S,A)\leq\mathsf{minProb}(\sup D,A)

Proof.

Since
S\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}\sup D
for all
S\in D
, then by
Lemma

B.9
we know that
\mathsf{minProb}(S,A)\leq\mathsf{minProb}(\sup D,A)
. Therefore, since the supremum is the least upper bound, it must be that
\sup_{S\in D}\mathsf{minProb}(S,A)\leq\mathsf{minProb}(\sup D,A)
.
∎

Lemma B.11 (Scott Continuity of
\mathsf{minProb}
).

For any directed set
D\subseteq\mathcal{C}(\mathsf{Mem}[S])
such that
\mu(A)=p
for all
\mu\in\sup D
:

\sup_{S\in D}\mathsf{minProb}(S,A)=\mathsf{minProb}(\sup D,A)

Proof.

By
Markowsky (1976, Corollary 3)
, we know that any chain-continuous function on a DCPO is Scott continuous, therefore it will suffice to show that
\mathsf{minProb}
is chain continuous.
Let
(S_{\delta})_{\delta<\zeta}
be a transfinite chain, so that
S_{\delta}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathsf{Mem}[V])
for all
\delta<\zeta
, and
S_{\delta}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}S_{\delta^{\prime}}
for all
\delta<\delta^{\prime}
. We will now prove that:

\sup_{\delta<\zeta}\mathsf{minProb}(S_{\delta},A)=\mathsf{minProb}\left(\sup_{\delta<\zeta}S_{\delta},A\right)

From
Lemma

B.10
, we already know that
\sup_{S\in D}\mathsf{minProb}(S,A)\leq\mathsf{minProb}(\sup D,A)
. We will now also show that
\sup_{S\in D}\mathsf{minProb}(S,A)<\mathsf{minProb}(\sup D,A)
is a contradiction, and therefore the two quantities are equal.
Let
p=\mathsf{minProb}(\sup_{\delta<\zeta}S_{\delta},A)
,
and suppose for the sake of contradiction that:

\sup_{\delta<\zeta}\mathsf{minProb}(S_{\delta},A)<\mathsf{minProb}(\sup_{\delta<\zeta}S_{\delta},A)

So, there is some
\varepsilon>0
such that
\sup_{\delta<\zeta}\mathsf{minProb}(S_{\delta},A)=p-\varepsilon
.

Let
T_{\delta}=\{\mu\in S_{\delta}\mid\mu(A)\leq p-\varepsilon\}
for all
\delta<\zeta
. If there exists a
\delta
such that
T_{\delta}=\emptyset
, then
\sup_{\delta^{\prime}<\zeta}\mathsf{minProb}(S_{\delta^{\prime}},A)\geq\mathsf{minProb}(S_{\delta},A)>p-\varepsilon
, which is a contradiction, therefore
T_{\delta}\neq\emptyset
for all
\delta<\zeta
. Further, by Lemma B.4.3 of
McIver and Morgan (2005)
, the
T_{\delta}
sets are closed, therefore their intersection must be nonempty by the finite intersection property. That means that there is some
\mu\in\bigcap_{\delta<\zeta}T_{\delta}\subseteq\bigcap_{\delta<\zeta}S_{\delta}=\sup_{\delta<\zeta}S_{\delta}
such that
\mu(A)\leq p-\varepsilon
, but this is a contradiction too since we know that
\mathsf{minProb}(\sup_{\delta<\zeta}S_{\delta},A)=p
. Therefore, it cannot be that
\sup_{\delta<\zeta}\mathsf{minProb}(S_{\delta},A)<\mathsf{minProb}(\sup_{\delta<\zeta}S_{\delta},A)
, and instead
\sup_{\delta<\zeta}\mathsf{minProb}(S_{\delta},A)=\mathsf{minProb}(\sup_{\delta<\zeta}S_{\delta},A)
.

∎

Lemma B.12.

\mathsf{minProb}(S\mathbin{\&}T,A)=\min(\mathsf{minProb}(S,A),\mathsf{minProb}(T,A))

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathsf{minProb}(S\mathbin{\&}T,A)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}(\{\mu\oplus_{p}\nu\mid\mu\in S,\nu\in T,p\in[0,1]\},A)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mu\in S}\inf_{\nu\in T}\inf_{p\in[0,1]}p\cdot\mu(A)+(1-p)\cdot\nu(A)

\displaystyle=\inf_{p\in[0,1]}p\cdot\left(\inf_{\mu\in S}\mu(A)\right)+(1-p)\cdot\left(\inf_{\nu\in T}\nu(A)\right)

\displaystyle=\inf_{p\in[0,1]}p\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(S,A)+(1-p)\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(T,A)

Now, there are three cases, if
\mathsf{minProb}(S,A)<\mathsf{minProb}(T,A)
, then the infimum occurs when
p=1
. If instead
\mathsf{minProb}(S,A)>\mathsf{minProb}(T,A)
, then the infimum occurs when
p=0
. If
\mathsf{minProb}(S,A)=\mathsf{minProb}(T,A)
, then the expression is equal for all
p
. So, the infimum always occurs at one of the extremes (
p=0
or
p=1
), and therefore:

\displaystyle=\min(\mathsf{minProb}(S,A),\mathsf{minProb}(T,A))

∎

Lemma B.13.

For any
f\colon\mathsf{Mem}[U]\to\mathcal{C}(\mathsf{Mem}[U])
,
S\in\mathcal{C}(\mathsf{Mem}[U])
, and
A\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[U]
:

\mathsf{minProb}(f^{\dagger}(S),A)=\inf_{\mu\in S}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)\cap\mathsf{Mem}[U]}\mu(\sigma)\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(f(\sigma),A)

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathsf{minProb}(f^{\dagger}(S),A)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}\left(\left\{\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(\sigma)\cdot\nu_{\sigma}~\middle|~\mu\in S,\forall\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu).\ \nu_{\sigma}\in f_{\bot}(\sigma)\right\},A\right)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mu\in S}\inf_{\nu_{\sigma}\in f_{\bot}(\sigma),\forall\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(\sigma)\cdot\nu_{\sigma}(A)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mu\in S}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(\sigma)\cdot\inf_{\nu_{\sigma}\in f_{\bot}(\sigma)}\nu_{\sigma}(A)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mu\in S}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(\sigma)\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(f_{\bot}(\sigma),A)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mu\in S}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)\cap\mathsf{Mem}[U]}\mu(\sigma)\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(f_{\bot}(\sigma),A)+\mu(\bot)\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(f_{\bot}(\bot),A)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mu\in S}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)\cap\mathsf{Mem}[U]}\mu(\sigma)\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(f(\sigma),A)+0

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mu\in S}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)\cap\mathsf{Mem}[U]}\mu(\sigma)\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(f(\sigma),A)

∎

Appendix C
Concurrency Lemmas

C.1.
Invariant Sensitive Execution

Lemma C.1 (Check Monotonicity).

For any
U,V,W\subseteq\mathsf{Var}
such that
U\cap V=\emptyset
,
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[U]
,
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[V]
, and
U\cup V\subseteq W
:

\mathsf{check}^{\mathcal{I}\ast\mathcal{J}}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}\mathsf{check}^{\mathcal{I}}

Proof.

Take any
\sigma\in\mathsf{Mem}[W]
. If
\pi_{U}(\sigma)\notin\mathcal{I}
or
\pi_{V}(\sigma)\notin\mathcal{J}
, then we get:

\mathsf{check}^{\mathcal{I}\ast\mathcal{J}}(\sigma)=\bot_{\mathcal{C}}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}\mathsf{check}^{\mathcal{I}}(\sigma)

So, we are done. If not, then
\pi_{U}(\sigma)\in\mathcal{I}
and
\pi_{V}(\sigma)\in\mathcal{J}
, so we get:

\mathsf{check}^{\mathcal{I}\ast\mathcal{J}}(\sigma)=\eta(\sigma)=\mathsf{check}^{\mathcal{I}}(\sigma)

∎

Lemma C.2 (Action Monotonicity).

For any
U,V,W\subseteq\mathsf{Var}
such that
U\cap V=\emptyset
,
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[U]
,
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[V]
, and
U\cup V\subseteq W
:

\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket^{\mathcal{I}\ast\mathcal{J}}_{\mathsf{Act}}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket^{\mathcal{I}}

Proof.

Let
W^{\prime}=W\setminus(U\cup V)
.
Take any
\sigma\in\mathsf{Mem}[W]
. if
\pi_{V}(\sigma)\notin\mathcal{J}
or
\pi_{U}(\sigma)\notin\mathcal{I}
, then:

\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket^{\mathcal{I}\ast\mathcal{J}}_{\mathsf{Act}}(\sigma)=\bot_{\mathcal{C}}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket^{\mathcal{I}}(\sigma)

So, we are done. If instead
\pi_{V}(\sigma)\in\mathcal{J}
and
\pi_{U}(\sigma)\in\mathcal{I}
, then we get:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket^{\mathcal{I}\ast\mathcal{J}}_{\mathsf{Act}}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{check}^{\mathcal{I}\ast\mathcal{J}})^{\dagger}\left(\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Act}}^{\dagger}\left((\mathsf{replace}^{\mathcal{I}\ast\mathcal{J}})^{\dagger}(\mathsf{check}^{\mathcal{I}\ast\mathcal{J}}(\sigma))\right)\right)

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{check}^{\mathcal{I}\ast\mathcal{J}})^{\dagger}\left(\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Act}}^{\dagger}\left(\mathsf{replace}^{\mathcal{I}\ast\mathcal{J}}(\sigma)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{check}^{\mathcal{I}\ast\mathcal{J}})^{\dagger}\left(\bignd_{\tau\in\mathcal{I}\ast\mathcal{J}}\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Act}}(\pi_{W^{\prime}}(\sigma)\uplus\tau)\right)

Since
\pi_{V}(\sigma)\in\mathcal{J}
and
\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}
is
\supseteq
.

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathsf{check}^{\mathcal{I}\ast\mathcal{J}})^{\dagger}\left(\bignd_{\tau\in\mathcal{I}}\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Act}}(\pi_{W^{\prime}\cup V}(\sigma)\uplus\tau)\right)

By
Lemma

C.1
and monotonicity of Kleisli extension.

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathsf{check}^{\mathcal{I}})^{\dagger}\left(\bignd_{\tau\in\mathcal{I}}\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Act}}(\pi_{W^{\prime}\cup V}(\sigma)\uplus\tau)\right)

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{check}^{\mathcal{I}})^{\dagger}\left(\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Act}}^{\dagger}\left(\mathsf{replace}^{\mathcal{I}}(\sigma)\right)\right)

Since
\pi_{U}(\sigma)\in\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{check}^{\mathcal{I}})^{\dagger}\left(\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Act}}^{\dagger}\left((\mathsf{replace}^{\mathcal{I}})^{\dagger}(\mathsf{check}^{\mathcal{I}}(\sigma))\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket^{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathsf{Act}}(\sigma)

∎

See
5.3

Proof.

For any pomset
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, let
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\mathcal{I}}
be the pomset obtained by replacing each action
a
with the tuple
\langle a,\mathcal{I}\rangle
. The evaluation function for these actions and order is as follows:

\left\llbracket\langle a,\mathcal{I}\rangle\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Act}}(\sigma)\triangleq\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket^{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathsf{Act}}(\sigma)\hskip 17.00024pt\langle a,\mathcal{I}\rangle\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{Act}}\langle a^{\prime},\mathcal{J}\rangle\hskip 8.50012pt\text{iff}\hskip 8.50012pta=a^{\prime}\hskip 8.50012pt\text{and}\hskip 8.50012pt\exists\mathcal{J}^{\prime}.\ \mathcal{I}=\mathcal{J}\ast\mathcal{J}^{\prime}

So, clearly
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\mathcal{I}\ast\mathcal{J}}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\mathcal{I}}
, and
\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\mathcal{I}})
. Note that
\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{Act}}
is not pointed or finitely proceeded, but this doesn’t matter; as we will see in the following proof, we only need this order for monotonicity, not for the extension lemma
(
Zilberstein
et al., 2025a
, Lemma 3.8)
.
We now complete the proof as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{I}\ast\mathcal{J}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\ll\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}^{\mathcal{I}\ast\mathcal{J}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\ll\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime\mathcal{I}\ast\mathcal{J}})

By Lemma H.3 of
Zilberstein
et al. (2025a)
and
Lemma

C.2
.

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}\sup_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\ll\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime\mathcal{I}})

\displaystyle=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\ll\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime})=\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})

∎

C.2.
Parallel Composition

Lemma C.3.

For any pairwise disjoint
U_{1},U_{2},V\subseteq\mathsf{Var}
,
\alpha
and
\beta
such that
\mathsf{vars}_{\mathsf{Act}}(\alpha)\subseteq U_{1}\cup V
,
\mathsf{vars}_{\mathsf{Test}}(\alpha)\subseteq U_{1}
,
\mathsf{vars}_{\mathsf{Act}}(\beta)\subseteq U_{2}\cup V
, and
\mathsf{vars}_{\mathsf{Test}}(\beta)\subseteq U_{2}
;
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[V]
,
S\subseteq N_{\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta}
,
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in\mathsk{form}
,
\sigma_{1}\in\mathsf{Mem}[U_{1}]
,
\sigma_{2}\in\mathsf{Mem}[U_{2}]
,
\tau\in\mathcal{I}
,
A\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[U_{1}]
, and
B\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[U_{2}]
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I})

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the size of
\mathsf{next}^{\star}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S)
. If the set is empty, then we have:

\displaystyle\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S)(\sigma_{2}\uplus_{2}\uplus\tau),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}(\eta(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma_{1}^{\prime}\in A}\sum_{\sigma_{2}^{\prime}\in B}\sum_{\tau^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}}\delta_{\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau}(\sigma_{1}^{\prime}\uplus\sigma_{2}^{\prime}\uplus\tau^{\prime})

Since we already know that
\tau\in\mathcal{I}
:

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma_{1}^{\prime}\in A}\sum_{\sigma_{2}^{\prime}\in B}\sum_{\tau^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}}\delta_{\sigma_{1}}(\sigma_{1}^{\prime})\cdot\delta_{\sigma_{2}}(\sigma_{2}^{\prime})\cdot\delta_{\tau}(\tau^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\sigma_{1}^{\prime}\in A}\delta_{\sigma_{1}}(\sigma_{1}^{\prime})\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{\sigma_{2}^{\prime}\in B}\delta_{\sigma_{2}}(\sigma_{2}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}\in A}\sum_{\tau^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}}\delta_{\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau}(\sigma_{1}^{\prime}\uplus\tau^{\prime})\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{\tau^{\prime}\in B}\sum_{\tau^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}}\delta_{\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau}(\sigma_{2}^{\prime}\uplus\tau^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}(\eta(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\eta(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau,B\ast\mathcal{I})

Now suppose that the set of next elements is not empty. If
\mathsf{next}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S)=\{x\}
, then we know that
\lambda_{\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta}(x)=\bullet
, and so:

\displaystyle\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S\cup\{x\})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I})

By the induction hypothesis:

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I})

If not, then
x
has already been scheduled and so
\mathsf{next}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S)=\mathsf{next}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha})\cup\mathsf{next}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})
.
Take any
y\in N_{\alpha}
, we will now show that:

\displaystyle\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S,y)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha},y)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I}\right)\cdot\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I}\right)

There are four cases:

(1)

\lambda_{\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta}(y)=\lambda_{\alpha}(y)\in\mathsf{Act}
. Let
a=\lambda_{\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta}(y)
, so:

\displaystyle\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S,y)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S\cup\{y\})^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Act}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau)),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I})

By
Lemma

B.13
.

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mu\in\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Act}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau)}\sum_{\rho\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)\cap\mathsf{Mem}[U_{1}\cup V]}\mu(\rho)\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S\cup\{y\})(\rho),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I})

Since
\mathsf{vars}(\alpha)\cap\mathsf{dom}(\sigma_{2})=\emptyset
.

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mu\in\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Act}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau)}\sum_{\rho\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)\cap\mathsf{Mem}[U_{1}\cup V]}\mu(\rho)\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S\cup\{y\})(\pi_{U_{1}}(\rho)\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\pi_{V}(\rho)),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I})

Note that by the definition of invariant sensitive execution, either
\pi_{V}(\rho)\subseteq\mathcal{I}
, or
\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Act}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau)=\bot_{\mathcal{C}}
. In the former case, we can apply the induction hypothesis. In the latter case, the entire expression must be zero, and therefore so is
\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S,y)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I})
, so the claim holds trivially. So, we presume the former is true and apply the induction hypothesis to get:

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mu\in\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Act}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau)}\sum_{\rho\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(\rho)\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha}\cup\{y\})(\rho),A\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\ast\pi_{V}(\rho)),B\ast\mathcal{I})

Note that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau)=\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau^{\prime})
for any
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}
, since the invariant sensitive execution reassigns the
V
variables at each step and tests only depend on local state.

\displaystyle=\left(\inf_{\mu\in\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Act}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau)}\sum_{\rho\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(\rho)\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha}\cup\{y\})(\rho),A\ast\mathcal{I})\right)\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau,B\ast\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\left(\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha}\cup\{y\})^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Act}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau)),A\ast\mathcal{I})\right)\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha},y)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}(\beta,\psi,S\cap N_{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I})

(2)

\lambda_{\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta}(y)=\lambda_{\alpha}(y)\in\mathsf{Test}
. Let
b=\lambda_{\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta}(y)
, and we therefore have:

\displaystyle\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S,y)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2}\land\llparenthesis y=\left\llbracket b\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Test}}(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau)\rrparenthesis,S\cup\{y\})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I})

By the induction hypothesis, and the fact that
\mathsf{vars}(b)\subseteq\mathsf{vars}_{\mathsf{Test}}(\alpha)\subseteq U_{1}
(therefore it does not depend on
\sigma_{2}\in\mathsf{Mem}[U_{2}]
and
\tau\in\mathsf{Mem}[V]
.

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi_{1}\land\llparenthesis y=\left\llbracket b\right\rrbracket(\sigma_{1})\rrparenthesis,S\cap N_{\alpha}\cup\{y\})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle\hskip 8.50012pt\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha},y)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I})

(3)

\lambda_{\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta}(y)=\lambda_{\alpha}(y)=\bot
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S,y)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}(\bot_{\mathcal{C}},A\ast B)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle=0\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha},y)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I})

(4)

\lambda_{\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta}(y)=\lambda_{\alpha}(y)=\bullet
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S,y)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S\cup\{y\})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I})

By the induction hypothesis.

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha}\cup\{y\})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha},y)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I})

By a nearly identical argument, we also get that for any
y\in N_{\beta}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S,y)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A)\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta},y)(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I})

We now complete the proof as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}\left(\bignd_{y\in\mathsf{next}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S)}\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S,y)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I}\right)

By
Lemma

B.12

\displaystyle={\min\limits_{y\in\mathsf{next}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S)}}\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S,y)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I}\right)

\displaystyle=\min\Big(\min_{y\in\mathsf{next}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha})}\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S,y)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I}),

\displaystyle\hskip 17.00024pt\min_{y\in\mathsf{next}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})}\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2},S,y)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I})\Big)

\displaystyle=\min\Big(\min_{y\in\mathsf{next}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha})}\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha},y)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I}),

\displaystyle\hskip 17.00024pt\min_{y\in\mathsf{next}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})}\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta},y)(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I})\Big)

\displaystyle=\min\Big(\left(\min_{y\in\mathsf{next}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha})}\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha},y)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I})\right)\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I}),

\displaystyle\hskip 17.00024pt\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot\left(\min_{y\in\mathsf{next}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})}\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta},y)(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I})\right)\Big)

\displaystyle=\min\Big(\mathsf{minProb}\left(\bignd_{y\in\mathsf{next}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha})}\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha},y)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I})\right)\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I}),

\displaystyle\hskip 17.00024pt\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot\mathsf{minProb}\left(\bignd_{y\in\mathsf{next}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})}\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{node}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta},y)(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I}\right)\Big)

\displaystyle=\min\Big(\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I}),

\displaystyle\hskip 17.00024pt\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I})\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\psi_{1},S\cap N_{\alpha})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\psi_{2},S\cap N_{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I})

∎

Lemma C.4.

For any pairwise disjoint
U_{1},U_{2},V\subseteq\mathsf{Var}
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}\in{\mathsk{pom}}_{\mathsf{fin}}
such that
\mathsf{vars}_{\mathsf{Act}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\subseteq U_{1}\cup V
,
\mathsf{vars}_{\mathsf{Test}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\subseteq U_{1}
,
\mathsf{vars}_{\mathsf{Act}}(\boldsymbol{\beta})\subseteq U_{2}\cup V
, and
\mathsf{vars}_{\mathsf{Test}}(\boldsymbol{\beta})\subseteq U_{2}
;
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[V]
,
\sigma_{1}\in\mathsf{Mem}[U_{1}]
,
\sigma_{2}\in\mathsf{Mem}[U_{2}]
,
\tau\in\mathcal{I}
,
A\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[U_{1}]
, and
B\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[U_{2}]
:

\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\parallel\boldsymbol{\beta})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I}\right)=\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I}\right)\cdot\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I}\right)

Proof.

Fix any
\alpha\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}
,
\beta\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
, and
x\notin N_{\alpha}\cup N_{\beta}
. This give us
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\parallel\boldsymbol{\beta}=[\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta]
. So, we get:

\displaystyle\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\parallel\boldsymbol{\beta})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}^{\mathcal{I}}([\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta])(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha\parallel_{x}\beta,\mathsf{true},\emptyset)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),A\ast B\ast\mathcal{I}\right)

By
Lemma

C.3
.

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha,\mathsf{true},\emptyset)(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I}\right)\cdot\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\beta,\mathsf{true},\emptyset)(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}^{\mathcal{I}}([\alpha])(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I}\right)\cdot\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}([\beta])(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),A\ast\mathcal{I}\right)\cdot\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpo}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B\ast\mathcal{I}\right)

∎

Lemma C.5.

For any pairwise disjoint
U_{1},U_{2},V\subseteq\mathsf{Var}
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}\in{\mathsk{pom}}
such that
\mathsf{vars}_{\mathsf{Act}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\subseteq U_{1}\cup V
,
\mathsf{vars}_{\mathsf{Test}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\subseteq U_{1}
,
\mathsf{vars}_{\mathsf{Act}}(\boldsymbol{\beta})\subseteq U_{2}\cup V
, and
\mathsf{vars}_{\mathsf{Test}}(\boldsymbol{\beta})\subseteq U_{2}
;
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[V]
,
\sigma_{1}\in\mathsf{Mem}[U_{1}]
,
\sigma_{2}\in\mathsf{Mem}[U_{2}]
,
\tau\in\mathcal{I}
,
A\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[U_{1}]
, and
B\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[U_{2}]
, if there exists
p
and
q
such that:

\forall\nu_{1}\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau).\ \nu_{1}(B_{1}\ast\mathcal{I})=p\hskip 17.00024pt\text{and}\hskip 17.00024pt\forall\nu_{2}\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau).\ \nu_{2}(B_{2}\ast\mathcal{I})=q

Then:

\forall\nu\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\parallel\boldsymbol{\beta})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau).\ \nu(B_{1}\ast B_{2}\ast\mathcal{I})\geq p\cdot q

Proof.

Take any
\nu\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\parallel\boldsymbol{\beta})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau)
. We have:

\displaystyle\nu(B_{1}\ast B_{2}\ast\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{minProb}(\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\parallel\boldsymbol{\beta})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B_{1}\ast B_{2}\ast\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}\left(\sup_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}\ll\boldsymbol{\alpha}\parallel\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B_{1}\ast B_{2}\ast\mathcal{I}\right)

By
Lemma

B.11
.

\displaystyle=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}\ll\boldsymbol{\alpha}\parallel\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B_{1}\ast B_{2}\ast\mathcal{I}\right)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\ll_{1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}\ll_{1}\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\parallel\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B_{1}\ast B_{2}\ast\mathcal{I}\right)

By
Lemma

C.4
.

\displaystyle=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\ll_{1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}\ll_{1}\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),B_{1}\ast\mathcal{I}\right)\cdot\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B_{2}\ast\mathcal{I}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sup_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\ll_{1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),B_{1}\ast\mathcal{I}\right)\right)\cdot\left(\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}\ll_{1}\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B_{2}\ast\mathcal{I}\right)\right)

By
Lemma

B.11
.

\displaystyle=\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})(\sigma_{1}\uplus\tau),B_{1}\ast\mathcal{I}\right)\cdot\mathsf{minProb}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\beta})(\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau),B_{2}\ast\mathcal{I}\right)

\displaystyle=p\cdot q

∎

Lemma C.6.

For any pairwise disjoint
U_{1},U_{2},V\subseteq\mathsf{Var}
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1},\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{2}\in{\mathsk{pom}}
such that
\mathsf{vars}_{\mathsf{Act}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k})\subseteq U_{k}\cup V
and
\mathsf{vars}_{\mathsf{Test}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k})\subseteq U_{k}
;
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathsf{Mem}[V]
,
let
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}
be the trivial probability space where
\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}}(\mathcal{I})=1
. For all
k\in\{1,2\}
,
\mathcal{P}_{k}
,
\mathcal{Q}_{k}
, and
\mu\in\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{Mem}[U_{1}\cup U_{2}\cup V])
such that
\mathcal{P}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\preceq\mu
, if:

\forall\mu_{k}.\ \mathcal{P}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\preceq\mu_{k}\implies\forall\nu_{k}\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k})^{\dagger}(\mu_{k}).\ \mathcal{Q}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\preceq\nu_{k}

Then:

\forall\nu\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}\parallel\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{2})^{\dagger}(\mu).\ \mathcal{Q}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{Q}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\preceq\nu

Proof.

Let
(A_{1,i})_{i\in I_{1}}
and
(A_{2,i})_{i\in I_{2}}
be the most precise, disjoint measurable events from
\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{P}_{1})
and
\mathsf{ev}(\mathcal{P}_{2})
, respectively. Since
\mathcal{P}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\preceq\mu
, we know that for any
(i,j)\in I_{1}\times I_{2}
:

(5)

\sum_{\sigma_{1}\in A_{1,i}}\sum_{\sigma_{2}\in A_{2,j}}\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{I}}\mu(\sigma_{1}\uplus\sigma_{2}\uplus\tau)=\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}(A_{1,i})\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}(A_{2,j})=\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}}(A_{1,i}\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{I}}(A_{2,j}\ast\mathcal{I})

Also, for any
\mu_{k}
such that
\mathcal{P}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\preceq\mu_{k}
, we know that any
\nu_{k}\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{I}}(\alpha_{k})^{\dagger}(\mu_{k})
has the form:

\nu_{k}=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu_{k})}\mu_{k}(\sigma)\cdot\nu_{\sigma}

Where each
\nu_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k})(\sigma)
. Since
\mathcal{Q}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\preceq\nu_{k}
, we know that for any
B\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}_{k}}
:

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{Q}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}}(B\ast\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in B\ast\mathcal{I}}\nu_{k}(\tau)=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu_{k})}\mu_{k}(\sigma)\cdot\sum_{\tau\in B\ast\mathcal{I}}\nu_{\sigma}(\tau)

Let
p_{B,\sigma}=\sum_{\tau\in B\ast\mathcal{I}}\nu_{\sigma}(\tau)
.

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu_{k})}\mu_{k}(\sigma)\cdot p_{B,\sigma}

We will now show that
\sum_{\tau\in B\ast\mathcal{I}}\xi(\tau)=p_{B,\sigma^{\prime}}
for any
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu_{k})
and
\xi\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k})(\sigma^{\prime})
. Take any such
\xi
. By construction,
\mu_{k}(\sigma^{\prime})\cdot\xi+\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu_{k})\setminus\{\sigma^{\prime}\}}\mu_{k}(\sigma)\cdot\nu_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k})^{\dagger}(\mu_{k})
, therefore:

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{Q}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}}(B\ast\mathcal{I})=\sum_{\tau\in B\ast\mathcal{I}}\left(\mu_{k}(\sigma^{\prime})\cdot\xi+\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu_{k})\setminus\{\sigma^{\prime}\}}\mu_{k}(\sigma)\cdot\nu_{\sigma}\right)(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu_{k})}\mu_{k}(\sigma)\cdot p_{B,\sigma}

\displaystyle\mu_{k}(\sigma^{\prime})\cdot\sum_{\tau\in B\ast\mathcal{I}}\xi(\tau)+\cancel{\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu_{k})\setminus\{\sigma^{\prime}\}}\mu_{k}(\sigma)\cdot p_{B,\sigma}}

\displaystyle=\mu_{k}(\sigma^{\prime})\cdot p_{B,\sigma^{\prime}}+\cancel{\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu_{k})\setminus\{\sigma^{\prime}\}}\mu_{k}(\sigma)\cdot p_{B,\sigma}}

\displaystyle\cancel{\mu_k(\sigma')}\cdot\sum_{\tau\in B\ast\mathcal{I}}\xi(\tau)

\displaystyle=\cancel{\mu_{k}(\sigma^{\prime})}\cdot p_{B,\sigma^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in B\ast\mathcal{I}}\xi(\tau)

\displaystyle=p_{B,\sigma^{\prime}}

Now, let
\mu_{k}
be constructed by fixing a single state
\sigma_{i}\in A_{k,i}\ast\mathcal{I}
for each
i\in I_{k}
and setting
\mu_{k}(\sigma_{i})=\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}}(A_{k,i})
, so clearly
\mathcal{P}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\preceq\mu_{k}
. Now let
p_{B,k,i}=p_{B,\sigma_{i}}
. Based on what we have just showed, this gives us:

\mu_{\mathcal{Q}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}}(B\ast\mathcal{I})=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu_{k})}\mu_{k}(\sigma)\cdot p_{B,\sigma}=\sum_{i\in I_{k}}\mu_{k}(\sigma_{i})\cdot p_{B,\sigma_{i}}=\sum_{i\in I_{k}}\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}}(A_{k,i}\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot p_{B,k,i}

We will now show that
p_{B,\sigma^{\prime}}=p_{B,k,j}
for any
j\in I_{k}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\in A_{k,j}\ast\mathcal{I}
. Take any such
\sigma^{\prime}
, then we get:

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{Q}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}}(B\ast\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}}(A_{k,j}\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot p_{B,\sigma^{\prime}}+\sum_{i\neq j}\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}}(A_{k,i}\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot p_{B,\sigma_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I_{k}}\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}}(A_{k,i}\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot p_{B,k,i}

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}}(A_{k,j}\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot p_{B,\sigma^{\prime}}+\cancel{\sum_{i\neq j}\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}}(A_{k,i}\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot p_{B,k,i}}

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}}(A_{k,j}\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot p_{B,k,j}+\cancel{\sum_{i\neq j}\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}}(A_{k,i}\ast\mathcal{I})\cdot p_{B,k,i}}

\displaystyle\cancel{\mu_{\mathcal{P}_k\otimes\mathcal{P}_\mathcal{I}}(A_{k,j}\ast\mathcal{I})}\cdot p_{B,\sigma^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\cancel{\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}}(A_{k,j}\ast\mathcal{I})}\cdot p_{B,k,j}

\displaystyle p_{B,\sigma^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=p_{B,k,j}

We have therefore shown that
\sum_{\tau\in B}\nu_{k}(\tau)=p_{B,i,k}
for any
\nu_{k}\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k})(\sigma)
where
B\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Q}_{k}}
,
i\in I_{k}
, and
\sigma\in A_{k,i}

Now take any
\nu\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}\parallel\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{2})^{\dagger}(\mu)
, which must have the form
\nu=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(\sigma)\cdot\nu_{\sigma}
where
\nu_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}\parallel\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{2})(\sigma)
for each
\sigma
. Since
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
operate over different address spaces, clearly
\mathcal{Q}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{Q}_{2}
exists. It just remains to show that
\mathcal{Q}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{Q}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\preceq\nu
, which we do as follows.
By
Lemma

B.2
