X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x_{2}\mapsto X^{\prime}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{own}(z)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~z\coloneqq\xor(x_{1},x_{2})~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bigoplus_{X^{\prime}\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}z\mapsto\xor(X,X^{\prime})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Split1}{\footnotesize{Split1}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{1}\mapsto X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast(x_{2}\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right))\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{own}(z)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~z\coloneqq\xor(x_{1},x_{2})~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}z\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{right=\!\!\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{\footnotesize{Consequence}}}

At this point, we have that
x_{1}
is deterministic and
x_{2}
is distributed according to a Bernoulli distribution.
We use
Split1
to do case analysis on the result of the sampling operation, at which point we use
Assign
to conclude that
z\mapsto\xor(X,X^{\prime})
. Since
X
is constant, then
\xor(X,X^{\prime})
is a bijection from
\{0,1\}
to
\{0,1\}
, and we can therefore use the rule of
Consequence
to conclude that
z
is uniformly distributed. We can now compose this information with the sampling operation immediately before.

(11)

\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{}{y\in\{0,1\}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{own}(x_{2})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x_{2}\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}x_{2}\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right)){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\enskip\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Samp}{\footnotesize{Samp}}}}{y\in\{0,1\}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{1}\mapsto X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{own}(x_{2},z)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x_{2}\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x_{1}\mapsto X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast(x_{2}\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right))\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{own}(z)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.24454pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Frame}{\footnotesize{Frame}}}\hskip 8.24454pt(\ref{eq:em-z})}{y\in\{0,1\}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{1}\mapsto X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{own}(x_{2},z)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x_{2}\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right)\fatsemi z\coloneqq\xor(x_{1},x_{2})~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}z\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Seq}{\footnotesize{Seq}}}}{y\in\{0,1\}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{own}(x_{2},z)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast\textstyle\bignd_{Y\in\{0,1\}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x_{1}\mapsto Y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x_{2}\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right)\fatsemi z\coloneqq\xor(x_{1},x_{2})~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}z\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{right={\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{\footnotesize{Consequence}}, \hyperlink{rule:NSplit2}{\footnotesize{NSplit2}}}}

We start by applying
NSplit2
in order to gain access to the nondeterministic value of
x_{1}
. It is important to do this
before
the sampling operation, since we need to ensure that
x_{2}
is uniformly distributed given any fixed value for
x_{1}
. Next, we apply
Seq
and derive specs for the individual commands. The sampling operation is simple, we complete the proof with the
Frame
and
Samp
rules. Now, we can derive a specification for the entire first thread in (
12
).

(12)

\frac{\frac{\frac{(\ref{eq:em-x1})}{y\in\{0,1\}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{own}(x_{1},x_{2},z)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x_{1}\coloneqq y~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{own}(x_{2},z)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast\textstyle\bignd_{Y\in\{0,1\}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x_{1}\mapsto Y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.24454pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Frame}{\footnotesize{Frame}}}\hskip 8.24454pt(\ref{eq:em-x2-z})}{y\in\{0,1\}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{own}(x_{1},x_{2},z)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x_{1}\coloneqq y\fatsemi x_{2}\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\fatsemi z\coloneqq\xor(x_{1},x_{2})~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}z\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Seq}{\footnotesize{Seq}}}}{y\in\{0,1\}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{own}(x_{1},x_{2},z)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x_{1}\coloneqq y\fatsemi x_{2}\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\fatsemi z\coloneqq\xor(x_{1},x_{2})~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}z\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Strengthen}{\footnotesize{Strengthen}}}

This step essentially just involves using
Seq
and
Frame
to compose the triples that we previously derived. At the end, we also use
Strengthen
, since the postcondition is now precise.
Finally, we complete the derivation for the whole program.

\hskip-68.00098pt\frac{\frac{\;}{\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{own}(x_{1},x_{2},y,z)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~y\coloneqq 0~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}y\mapsto 0\ast\mathsf{own}(x_{1},x_{2},z)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Assign}{\footnotesize{Assign}}}\frac{\frac{\frac{(\ref{eq:em-t1})\enskip\frac{\frac{\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y\in\{0,1\}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~y\coloneqq 1~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}y\mapsto 1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\enskip\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Assign}{\footnotesize{Assign}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y\in\{0,1\}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~y\coloneqq 1~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}y\in\{0,1\}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\enskip\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{\footnotesize{Consequence}}}}{y\in\{0,1\}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~y\coloneqq 1~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\enskip\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Atom}{\footnotesize{Atom}}}}{y\in\{0,1\}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{own}(x_{1},x_{2},z)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x_{1}\coloneqq y\fatsemi x_{2}\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\fatsemi z\coloneqq\xor(x_{1},x_{2})\parallel y\coloneqq 1~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}z\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\enskip\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Par}{\footnotesize{Par}}}}{\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{own}(x_{1},x_{2},z)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}y\in\{0,1\}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x_{1}\coloneqq y\fatsemi x_{2}\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\fatsemi z\coloneqq\xor(x_{1},x_{2})\parallel y\coloneqq 1~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}(z\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right))\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}y\in\{0,1\}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.24454pt\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Share}{\footnotesize{Share}}}}{\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y\mapsto 0\ast\mathsf{own}(x_{1},x_{2},z)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x_{1}\coloneqq y\fatsemi x_{2}\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\fatsemi z\coloneqq\xor(x_{1},x_{2})\parallel y\coloneqq 1~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}z\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{\footnotesize{Consequence}},vdots=3em,leftskip=20em,rightskip=10em}}{\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{own}(x_{1},x_{2},y,z)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~y\coloneqq 0\fatsemi(x_{1}\coloneqq y\fatsemi x_{2}\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\fatsemi z\coloneqq\xor(x_{1},x_{2})\parallel y\coloneqq 1)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}z\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Seq}{\footnotesize{Seq}}}

After concluding that
y\mapsto 0
after the first command, we can weaken this information and use
Share
to move
y
into the invariant. Next, we use
Par
to compositionally analyze the two threads. We have already seen the proof of the first thread above. The second thread has a simple derivation, since
y\coloneqq 1
is clearly atomic and obeys the invariant.

F.4.
Concurrent Shuffling

Recall the following program from
Section

6.2
for concurrent shuffling a list using two parallel threads.

\begin{array}[]{ll}\begin{array}[]{l}a_{1}\coloneqq[]\fatsemi a_{2}\coloneqq[]\fatsemi i\coloneqq 0\fatsemi\\
\texttt{while}~i<\texttt{len}(a)~\texttt{do}~(\\
\hskip 8.50012ptb\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\fatsemi\\
\hskip 8.50012pt\texttt{if}~b~\texttt{then}~a_{1}\coloneqq a_{1}\mathbin{+\!+}[a[i]]~\texttt{else}~a_{2}\coloneqq a_{2}\mathbin{+\!+}[a[i]]\fatsemi\\
\hskip 8.50012pti\coloneqq i+1\\
)\fatsemi\\
\texttt{shuffle}_{1}\parallel\texttt{shuffle}_{2}\fatsemi\\
a\coloneqq a_{1}\mathbin{+\!+}a_{2}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{shuffle}_{k}:\\
\hskip 8.50012pti_{k}\coloneqq\texttt{len}(a_{k})-1\fatsemi\\
\hskip 8.50012pt\texttt{while}~i_{k}>0~\texttt{do}\\
\hskip 17.00024ptj_{k}\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{unif}\left([0,\ldots,i_{k}]\right)\fatsemi\\
\hskip 17.00024pta_{k}\coloneqq\texttt{swap}(a_{k},i_{k},j_{k})\fatsemi\\
\hskip 17.00024pti_{k}\coloneqq i_{k}-1\end{array}\end{array}

We now give the complete derivation of the correctness proof. We begin with the
\texttt{shuffle}_{k}
program. To define the loop invariant, we first recursively define
\texttt{swaps}(n,\ell)
which gives the set of possible lists obtained when
i_{k}\mapsto n
. It is easy to see that
\texttt{swaps}(0,\ell)=\Pi(\ell)
, since once
i
reaches
n
, all permutations of the elements above position
n
are accounted for, so once
i
reaches 0, all permutations of the entire list are accounted for (the final position does not explicitly need to be chosen, since only one element remains).

\texttt{swaps}(n,\ell)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{\ell\}&\text{if}~n=\texttt{len}(\ell)-1\\
\{\texttt{swap}(\ell^{\prime},n+1,j)\mid\ell^{\prime}\in\texttt{swaps}(n+1,\ell),j\in\{0,\ldots,n+1\}\}&\text{if}~0\leq n<\texttt{len}(\ell)-1\end{array}\right.

We now define the loop invariant
\varphi
in using
swaps
. The rank
R
is given by
i_{k}
, which is bounded between
0
and
\texttt{len}(A)-1
. The final postcondition
\varphi[0/R]
can also be simplified as follows.

\varphi\triangleq\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i_{k}\mapsto R\ast 0\leq R\leq\texttt{len}(A)-1\ast\mathsf{own}(j_{k})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast(a_{k}\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\texttt{swaps}(R,A)\right))\hskip 17.00024pt\varphi[0/R]=\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}i_{k}\mapsto 0\ast\mathsf{own}(j_{k})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast(a_{k}\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\Pi(A)\right))

We now show the derivation for the loop body as a decorated program:

(13)

\begin{array}[]{ll}&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R=N>0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{\textsc{Consequence}}&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i_{k}\mapsto N\ast 0<N\leq\texttt{len}(A)-1\ast\mathsf{own}(j_{k})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast(a_{k}\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\texttt{swaps}(\texttt{len}(A)-1-N,A)\right)){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\hyperlink{rule:Samp}{\textsc{Samp}}&\hskip 8.50012ptj_{k}\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{unif}\left([0,\ldots,i_{k}]\right)\fatsemi\\
&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i_{k}\mapsto N\ast 0<N\leq\texttt{len}(A)-1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast(a_{k}\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\texttt{swaps}(N,A)\right))\ast(j_{k}\sim\mathbf{unif}\left([0,\ldots,N]\right){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{\textsc{Consequence}}&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{X\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\texttt{swaps}(N,A)\right)}\bigoplus_{Y\sim\mathbf{unif}\left([0,\ldots,N]\right)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i_{k}\mapsto N\ast 0<N\leq\texttt{len}(A)-1\ast a_{k}\mapsto X\ast j_{k}\mapsto Y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\hyperlink{rule:Split1}{\textsc{Split1}},\hyperlink{rule:Assign}{\textsc{Assign}}&\hskip 8.50012pta_{k}\coloneqq\texttt{swap}(a_{k},i_{k},j_{k})\fatsemi\\
&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{X\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\texttt{swaps}(N,A)\right)}\bigoplus_{Y\sim\mathbf{unif}\left([0,\ldots,N]\right)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i_{k}\mapsto N\ast 0<N\leq\texttt{len}(A)-1\ast a_{k}\mapsto\texttt{swap}(X,N,Y)\ast j_{k}\mapsto Y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{\textsc{Consequence}}&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i_{k}\mapsto N\ast 0<N\leq\texttt{len}(A)-1\ast\mathsf{own}(j_{k})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast(a_{k}\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\texttt{swaps}(N-1,A)\right)){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\hyperlink{rule:Assign}{\textsc{Assign}}&\hskip 8.50012pti_{k}\coloneqq i_{k}-1\\
&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i_{k}\mapsto N-1\ast 0<N\leq\texttt{len}(A)-1\ast\mathsf{own}(j_{k})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast(a_{k}\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\texttt{swaps}(N-1,A)\right)){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{\textsc{Consequence}}&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}(\bignd_{R=0}^{N-1}\varphi)\oplus_{1}(\bignd_{R=N}^{\texttt{len}(A)-1}\varphi){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\end{array}

When entering the loop body, we know that the tail of
a_{k}
is already shuffled. After performing the swap of
i_{k}
and
j_{k}
, we extend the shuffled tail by one position. Clearly, the shuffles remain uniformly distributed, since
X
is uniformly distributed, and the element at position
N
is also chosen uniformly.
Now, we give the remaining derivation of
\texttt{shuffle}_{k}
, which is quite mechanical given the derivation of the loop body above:

(14)

\frac{\frac{\;}{\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a_{k}\mapsto A\ast\mathsf{own}(i_{k},j_{k})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~i_{k}\coloneqq\texttt{len}(a_{k})-1~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}a_{k}\mapsto A\ast i_{k}\mapsto\texttt{len}(A)-1\ast\mathsf{own}(j_{k})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Assign}{\footnotesize{Assign}},vdots=6em,rightskip=30em}\frac{\frac{(\ref{eq:fy-body})}{\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bignd_{R=0}^{\texttt{len}(A)-1}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{while}~i_{k}>0~\texttt{do}~(\cdots)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}(a_{k}\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\Pi(A)\right))\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i_{k}\mapsto 0\ast\mathsf{own}(j_{k})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip We now move to deriving the specification for the main program. To analyze the loop, we use the following loop invariant, where
M=\texttt{len}(A)
. Clearly, the rank
R
is bounded between
0
and
M
, and when
R=0
the loop must terminate since
i=\texttt{len}(a)
.

\psi=\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i\mapsto M-R\ast 0\leq R\leq M\ast a\mapsto A\ast\mathsf{own}(b)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast\bigoplus_{X\sim\mathbf{unif}\left(\{0,1\}^{M-R}\right)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}a_{1}\mapsto A[X]\ast a_{2}\mapsto A[\neg X]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil

We now show the derivation of the loop body as a decorated program. The key idea is to merge the newly sampled value of
b
into the
\bigoplus
over
X
to extend the length of the bit-string on each iteration.

F.5.
Private Information Retrieval

Recall the following private information retrieval program from
Section

6.3
.

\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{PrivFetch}:\\
\hskip 8.50012ptq_{1}\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{unif}\left(\{0,1\}^{n}\right)\fatsemi\\
\hskip 8.50012ptq_{2}\coloneqq\xor(q_{1},x)\fatsemi\\
\hskip 8.50012pt\texttt{fetch}_{1}\parallel\texttt{fetch}_{2}\fatsemi\\
\hskip 8.50012ptr\coloneqq\xor(r_{1},r_{2})\\
\end{array}\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{fetch}_{k}:\\
\hskip 8.50012pti_{k}\coloneqq 0\fatsemi r_{k}\coloneqq 0\fatsemi\\
\hskip 8.50012pt\texttt{while}~i_{k}<\texttt{len}(q_{k})~\texttt{do}\\
\hskip 17.00024pt\texttt{if}~q_{k}[i_{k}]=1~\texttt{then}\\
\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 8.50012ptr_{k}\coloneqq\xor(r_{k},d[i_{k}])\fatsemi\\
\hskip 17.00024pti_{k}\coloneqq i_{k}+1\end{array}

We now give the complete correctness proof, showing that
PrivFetch
correctly fetches the data entry indicated by
x
from the database. We begin with the
\texttt{fetch}_{k}
procedure, whose proof is shown below in (
16
).

(16)

\frac{\frac{\;}{d\mapsto D\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q_{k}\mapsto Q\ast\mathsf{own}(i_{k},r_{k})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~i_{k}\coloneqq 0~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}q_{k}\mapsto Q\ast i_{k}\mapsto 0\ast\mathsf{own}(r_{k})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Assign}{\footnotesize{Assign}}}\frac{\frac{\;}{d\mapsto D\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q_{k}\mapsto Q\ast i_{k}\mapsto 0\ast\mathsf{own}(r_{k})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~r_{k}\coloneqq 0~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}q_{k}\mapsto Q\ast i_{k}\mapsto 0\ast r_{k}\mapsto 0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.24454pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Assign}{\footnotesize{Assign}}}\hskip 8.24454pt(\ref{eq:pir-loop})}{d\mapsto D\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q_{k}\mapsto Q\ast i_{k}\mapsto 0\ast\mathsf{own}(r_{k})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~r_{k}\coloneqq 0\fatsemi\texttt{while}~i_{k}<\texttt{len}(q_{k})~\texttt{do}~(\cdots)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}r_{k}\mapsto\xor_{0\leq i<n:Q[i]=1}D[i]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Seq}{\footnotesize{Seq}},vdots=4em,leftskip=20em}}{d\mapsto D\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q_{k}\mapsto Q\ast\mathsf{own}(i_{k},r_{k})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~i_{k}\coloneqq 0\fatsemi r_{k}\coloneqq 0\fatsemi\texttt{while}~i_{k}<\texttt{len}(q_{k})~\texttt{do}~(\cdots)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}r_{k}\mapsto\xor_{0\leq i<n:Q[i]=1}D[i]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Seq}{\footnotesize{Seq}}}

The proof above is quite mechanical, just using
Seq
and
Assign
to analyze the initialization. The next step is to analyze the loop. To do so, we use the following invariant, which states that after
i_{k}
iterations,
r_{k}
holds a dot product of the first
i_{k}
elements of
D
and
Q
.

\varphi\triangleq\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q_{k}\mapsto Q\ast i_{k}\mapsto n-R\ast 0\leq R\leq n\ast r_{k}\mapsto\xor_{0\leq i<n-R:Q[i]=1}D[i]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil

The rank
R
indicates the remaining number of iterations until termination, and it clearly bounded between 0 and
n
. We can also specialize
\varphi
to obtain the pre- and postconditions of the whole loop, shown below:

\varphi[n/R]=\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q_{k}\mapsto Q\ast i_{k}\mapsto 0\ast r_{k}\mapsto 0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt\varphi[0/R]=\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}q_{k}\mapsto Q\ast i_{k}\mapsto n-R\ast r_{k}\mapsto\xor_{0\leq i<n:Q[i]=1}D[i]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil

Analyzing the loop is simply a matter of applying
BoundedRank
.

(17)

\frac{\frac{(\ref{eq:pir-loop-body})}{d\mapsto D\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bignd_{R=0}^{n}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{while}~i_{k}<\texttt{len}(q_{k})~\texttt{do}~(\cdots)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi[0/R]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:BoundedRank}{\footnotesize{BoundedRank}}}}{d\mapsto D\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q_{k}\mapsto Q\ast i_{k}\mapsto 0\ast r_{k}\mapsto 0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{while}~i_{k}<\texttt{len}(q_{k})~\texttt{do}~(\cdots)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}r_{k}\mapsto\xor_{0\leq i<n:Q[i]=1}D[i]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{\footnotesize{Consequence}}}

allows us to extend the dot product.

(21)

\frac{\frac{\;}{\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r_{1}\mapsto\!\!\!\xor_{0\leq i<n:Q[i]=1}\!\!\!D[i]\ast r_{2}\mapsto\!\!\!\!\!\!\xor_{0\leq i<n:\xor(Q,\texttt{onehot}(K))[i]=1}\!\!\!\!\!\!D[i]\ast\mathsf{own}(r)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~r\coloneqq\xor(r_{1},r_{2})~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}r\mapsto D[K]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Assign}{\footnotesize{Assign}}}}{\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r_{1}\mapsto\!\!\!\xor_{0\leq i<n:Q[i]=1}\!\!\!D[i]\ast r_{2}\mapsto\!\!\!\!\!\!\xor_{0\leq i<n:\xor(Q,\texttt{onehot}(K))[i]=1}\!\!\!\!\!\!D[i]\ast d\mapsto D\ast\mathsf{own}(r)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~r\coloneqq\xor(r_{1},r_{2})~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}r\mapsto D[K]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{\footnotesize{Consequence}}}

Below, we show why
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xor(r_{1},r_{2})\mapsto D[K]\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
:

\displaystyle\xor\left(\xor_{0\leq i<n:Q[i]=1}D[i],\xor_{0\leq i<n:\xor(Q,\texttt{onehot}(K))[i]=1}D[i]\right)

\displaystyle=\xor_{i=0}^{n-1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\xor(D[i],D[i])&\text{if}~Q[i]=\xor(Q,\texttt{onehot}(K))[i]=1\\
D[i]&\text{if}~Q[i]=\neg\xor(Q,\texttt{onehot}(K))[i]\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

Since
\xor(D[i],D[i])=0
, we can combine the first and last cases.

\displaystyle=\xor_{i=0}^{n-1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}D[i]&\text{if}~Q[i]=\neg\xor(Q,\texttt{onehot}(K))[i]\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

Q[i]=\neg\xor(Q,\texttt{onehot}(K))[i]
occurs only when
i=K
.

\displaystyle=\xor_{i=0}^{n-1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}D[i]&\text{if}~i=K\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

Since
\xor(x,0)=x
, then we can drop all the zero terms.

\displaystyle=D[K]

F.6.
The von Neumann Trick

Recall the following program from
Section

6.4
, which simulates a fair coin given a coin whose bias can be altered by a parallel thread.

\begin{array}[]{l}x\coloneqq 0\fatsemi\\
y\coloneqq 0\fatsemi\\
\texttt{while}~x=y~\texttt{do}\\
\hskip 8.50012ptp^{\prime}\coloneqq p\fatsemi\\
\hskip 8.50012ptx\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(p^{\prime}\right)\fatsemi\\
\hskip 8.50012pty\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(p^{\prime}\right)\end{array}

In this section, we provide the complete derivation for the correctness of this program, showing both that it almost surely terminates, and also that
x
is distributed like a fair coin flip at the end of the program execution. We are going to use the resource invariant
I\triangleq(p\in\ell)
, where
\ell
is a finite list of values between
\varepsilon
and
1-\varepsilon
, with
0<\varepsilon\leq\frac{1}{2}
.
For the purposes of analyzing the while loop, we will use the loop invariant
\varphi
below:

\varphi\triangleq\varphi_{0}\vee\varphi_{1}\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt\varphi_{0}\triangleq\bigoplus_{X\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\mapsto X\ast y\mapsto\lnot X\ast R=0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt\varphi_{1}\triangleq\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=y\mapsto\mathsf{true}\ast R=1\ast\mathsf{own}(p^{\prime})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil

Note that this gives us
\varphi\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=y\mapsto R\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, so when
R=0
the loop will terminate. Clearly, this also implies that
R
is bounded between 0 and 1. In addition,
\varphi[0/R]=\bigoplus_{X\in\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\mapsto X\ast y\mapsto\lnot X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
and it is precise.
We start with the derivation of the command
p\coloneqq p^{\prime}
in (
22
), which reads a value from shared state. This derivation proceeds by using the
Atom
rule to open the invariant and the
Exists
rule to get the specific value of
p^{\prime}
at the point of the read.

(22)

\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\;}{\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p\mapsto X\ast\mathsf{own}(x,y,p^{\prime})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~p^{\prime}\coloneqq p~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bignd_{X\in\ell}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}p^{\prime}\mapsto X\ast p\mapsto X\ast\mathsf{own}(x,y)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.24454pt\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:NSplit1}{\footnotesize{NSplit1}}}}{\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=y\mapsto\mathsf{true}\ast R=1\ast\mathsf{own}(p^{\prime})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}p\in\ell\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~p^{\prime}\coloneqq 8.50012pt\textsc{Right={\hyperlink{rule:Exists}{\footnotesize{Exists}}+\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{\footnotesize{Consequence}}}}}{p\in\ell\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=y\mapsto\mathsf{true}\ast p~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bignd_{X\in\ell}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}p^{\prime}\mapsto X\ast\mathsf{own}(x,y)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Atom}{\footnotesize{Atom}}}

Next, we derive a specification for the two sampling operations in (
24
). First, the
NSplit2
rule is used to compositionally reason about the nondeterministic outcomes. In the premise of that rule, the value of
p^{\prime}
is deterministic, so that
Samp
can be used twice for the writes to
x
and
y
. In the end,
x
and
y
are independently and identically distributed according to
\mathbf{Ber}\left(X\right)
. We complete the proof using the rule of
Consequence
, with implication (
23
).

(23)

\begin{split}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p^{\prime}\mapsto X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast(x\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(X\right))\ast(y\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(X\right))&\implies\textstyle\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p^{\prime}\mapsto X\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\ast\left(\bigoplus_{Y\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(X\right)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\mapsto Y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\right)\ast\left(\bigoplus_{Z\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(X\right)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}y\mapsto Z\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\right)\\
&\implies\textstyle\bigoplus_{Y\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(X\right)}\bigoplus_{Z\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(X\right)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p^{\prime}\mapsto X\ast x\mapsto Y\ast y\mapsto Z\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\\
&\implies\textstyle\left(\bigoplus_{Y\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p^{\prime}\mapsto X\ast x\mapsto Y\ast y\mapsto\lnot Y\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\right)\oplus_{2X(1-X)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=y\mapsto\mathsf{true}\ast\mathsf{own}(p^{\prime})\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\\
&\implies\varphi_{0}\oplus_{2X(1-X)}\varphi_{1}\\
&\implies\varphi_{0}\oplus_{\geq 2\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)}\varphi_{1}\end{split}

Now, the derivation is shown below.

(24)

\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\;}{I\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p^{\prime}\mapsto X\ast\mathsf{own}(x,y)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(p^{\prime}\right)\fatsemi 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt

Note that the use of
NSplit2
results in a weak triple, and given that the postcondition of (
24
) is not precise, it is not possible to use a strong version of the rule. However, after the loop terminates, the postcondition
is
precise, and so we can use
Strengthen
later, as seen in (
25
). Note that for the derivation of the loop body, the only possibility for
R>0
is
R=1
, so
\varphi\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R=N>0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
is equivalent to
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=y\mapsto\mathsf{true}\ast\mathsf{own}(p^{\prime})\ast R=N=1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil=\varphi_{1}\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}N=1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
. Now, letting
p=2\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)
be the minimum probability that the rank decreases, the premise of
BoundedRank
requires us to show that
\varphi_{0}
occurs with probability at least
p
. This derivation is shown below.

(25)

\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{(\ref{eq:vn-inv})\enskip(\ref{eq:vn-samp})}{I\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{1}\ast\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N=1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~p^{\prime}\coloneqq y\mathbin{:\approx}\mathbf{Ber}\left(p^{\prime}\right)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}x\sim\mathbf{Ber}\left(1/2\right){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{\footnotesize{Consequence}}}

Finally, we include the initial assignments to
x
and
y
to complete the proof.
